CHAPTER 16


Decryption


The heavy steel door of the basement stairwell slammed shut behind Atwater Briggs, the thick deadbolt engaging with a resounding, metallic clack that severed him entirely from the adrenaline-soaked chaos of the living room above.


The silence of the subterranean corridor was absolute, a heavy, pressurized vacuum that instantly grounded his racing pulse. Atwater stood motionless for three precise seconds, allowing the sensory data of his environment to overwrite the visceral memory of Darell’s torn flesh and Callie’s blood-stained hands. The air down here was artificially chilled, scrubbed of moisture and particulates by the heavy-duty HVAC system, leaving it tasting faintly of copper and ozone.


He looked down at his right hand. Resting in the center of his palm, slick with the faint, tacky residue of his brother’s blood, was the matte-black plastic casing of the encrypted thumb drive.


It weighed less than an ounce, yet it contained enough localized gravity to collapse a multi-billion-dollar international syndicate.


Atwater closed his fingers around the drive and began walking down the long, gray cinderblock hall. His boots made no sound against the sealed concrete. He bypassed the holding cell where Callie had spent her first forty-eight hours, moving directly toward the heavily reinforced, windowless steel door at the absolute end of the corridor.


This was the Tech Hub. It was his sanctuary, his armory, and the centralized nervous system of the Copper Compound. While Darell dictated the physical boundaries of their empire with lead and cordwood, Atwater dictated their digital invisibility.


He reached the door and pressed his thumb against the biometric scanner mounted flush against the steel frame. A thin beam of red light swept across his print. The mechanism chimed—a low, harmonious two-note confirmation—followed by the heavy, mechanical thud of six internal locking tumblers sliding backward in perfect unison.


Atwater pushed the door open and stepped into the dark.


The room woke up the moment he crossed the threshold, motion sensors triggering the localized power grid. The primary overhead lights remained off, but the perimeter baseboards ignited with a low, ambient blue glow. Along the far wall, three towering, custom-built server racks hummed to life, the internal cooling fans spooling up with a sound like a distant, idling jet turbine. The racks were a masterpiece of illicit engineering, utilizing liquid-cooling loops to manage the extreme thermal output of the high-end processing units stacked within.


In the center of the room sat his workstation: a massive, matte-black desk supporting an array of six curved, high-resolution monitors configured in a seamless, panoramic arc.


The smell of the room was intensely familiar. It was the scent of hot silicon, sterile recycled air, and the bitter, acidic tang of week-old dark roast coffee permanently ingrained into the fabric of the ergonomic chair.


Atwater approached the desk and deposited the blood-smudged drive onto the pristine black surface.


He did not immediately sit down. He moved to the small kitchenette station installed in the corner of the room, switching on the heavy-duty espresso machine. While the boiler hissed and sputtered, bringing the water to optimal extraction temperature, Atwater walked over to the primary server rack.


Before he could introduce the cartel’s payload to his systems, he had to sever the hollow from the rest of the world.


He opened the glass door of the server cabinet and located the primary fiber-optic trunk line that provided the compound with its heavily encrypted, bounce-routed internet connection. He grasped the thick blue cable and physically unclipped it from the central router.


The network status lights on the switch immediately flashed from steady green to a rapid, warning amber.


The Copper Compound was now entirely air-gapped. No signal could leave. No signal could enter. If Miriam Daniel’s digital security architects possessed a tracking algorithm embedded within the drive's firmware designed to ping their home servers the moment it received power, the signal would hit a dead, impenetrable wall of disconnected hardware. They were in a digital quarantine.


Atwater closed the server cabinet, poured a double shot of espresso into a heavy ceramic mug, and carried it back to his desk.


He sat down, adjusting his wire-rimmed glasses, and woke the workstation. The six monitors flared to life, casting a stark, bluish-white light across his sharp, angular features. He initiated a specialized, isolated virtual machine—a digital sandbox designed to open hostile files without allowing them access to the host computer’s core operating system.


He picked up the thumb drive, wiping the last smear of Darell’s blood off the casing with the edge of his t-shirt, and slotted it into a dedicated, heavily monitored USB terminal on the desktop console.


The system registered the hardware instantly.


A single, stark prompt appeared in the center of the primary monitor: Encrypted Volume Detected. Enter Passkey.


Atwater did not have the passkey. He took a slow sip of the scalding espresso, letting the bitter caffeine hit his empty stomach, and began to type. His fingers moved across the mechanical keyboard with blistering, flawless speed, the clatter of the keys a rapid, rhythmic drumbeat in the quiet room.


He pulled up a diagnostic diagnostic tool, directing it to analyze the architecture of the encryption wrapper protecting the drive. Lines of code cascaded down the secondary monitor, analyzing the file system, the bit-locker parameters, and the algorithmic density of the lock.


It took the diagnostic tool four minutes to spit out the summary.


Atwater read the glowing text, his jaw tightening into a hard, inflexible line.


Algorithm: AES-256-GCM. Key Derivation: PBKDF2 with 500,000 iterations. Salt: Randomized 256-bit.


It was military-grade. It was the exact type of cryptographic armor utilized by federal intelligence agencies to protect state secrets. It was a lock that could not be picked, guessed, or bypassed through standard backdoor vulnerabilities. The mathematics involved were absolute. A standard, commercially available computer would take several billion years to cycle through the possible combinations required to randomly guess the correct 256-bit key.


But Atwater Briggs did not rely on the mathematically impossible fantasy of raw brute force. Attempting to batter down an AES-256 wall with a dictionary attack, even utilizing the unmitigated processing power of his three liquid-cooled server racks, would take his hardware several billion years. He couldn't break the lock. He had to exploit the locksmith.


He swiveled his ergonomic chair to the left, his fingers flying across a secondary mechanical keyboard linked directly to his specialized diagnostic partition. He wasn't going to attack the encryption wrapper directly; he was going to execute a highly invasive side-channel memory dump on the drive's firmware controller. Genevieve Hersey was a brilliant corporate architect, but she was still a corporate accountant. Corporate accountants relied on standardized, rotational password matrices. They reused structural logic. They recycled localized data salts.


Atwater began writing a customized, predatory zero-day script designed to sift through the microscopic, residual cache memory left behind on the thumb drive’s physical flash sectors. He networked the raw, thermal-heavy processing power of his specialized graphics units, directing them to parse the firmware dump for the specific, predictable alphanumeric structures Miriam Daniel's holding firm utilized for their administrative logins. He wasn't throwing a sledgehammer at the door; he was using a digital scalpel to extract the spare key the architect had carelessly left under the digital welcome mat.


He finalized the invasive script, routing one hundred percent of the compound's computational resources toward reverse-engineering the key derivation salt. The localized lights in the subterranean basement dimmed for a terrifying fraction of a second as the massive power draw kicked in. The heavy liquid-cooling fans in the towering racks screamed, elevating from a low, rhythmic hum to a frantic, high-pitched whine. The processors immediately redlined, generating a massive, suffocating wave of thermal exhaust that instantly raised the ambient temperature of the room.


The primary monitor blinked, the stark white text displaying a simple, minimalist progress bar beneath the words: Firmware Cache Extraction: Active. Side-Channel Vulnerability Identified. Reverse-Engineering Key Matrix.


Now, there was nothing to do but bleed the servers dry and wait.


Atwater leaned back in his chair, peeling his glasses off his face. He pinched the bridge of his nose, feeling the deep, throbbing ache of chronic exhaustion threatening to breach his focus.


He looked at the digital clock in the corner of the screen. 1:14 AM.


The quiet of the air-gapped room allowed his analytical mind to momentarily drift back to the living room upstairs. The geometry of the syndicate had fundamentally altered in the span of a single afternoon. Darell was injured. The absolute anonymity of the hollow was burned. The cartel was physically moving through the woods, closing the net.


And Callie.


Atwater thought about the woman who had knelt on the hot stone hearth, smashing her own boot to pieces to hand them the weapon they needed to survive. When Darell had first carried her into the basement, Atwater had calculated her existence purely as a liability. A variable that introduced unacceptable risk. He had advocated for her immediate elimination to protect the family.


But Callie had systematically dismantled his logic. She hadn't cowered in the face of their violence; she had adapted to it. She had recognized the structural integrity of the Duncan brotherhood and deliberately woven herself into the fabric of it. By surrendering the drive, she had permanently transitioned from a piece of contested property to an active, essential stakeholder in their war.


She had bought her seat at the table with blood and intelligence. Atwater respected intelligence above all else.


He put his glasses back on and refocused on the screens.


The progress bar had moved a fraction of a millimeter. The estimated time to completion was wildly fluctuating, jumping from three hours to six days, dependent on the complexity of the specific password string the algorithm was currently hammering against.


Atwater did not sleep. He could not afford the luxury of unconsciousness while the perimeter was under threat and the key to their survival was locked behind a digital wall.


He spent the next three hours performing maintenance on the script, optimizing the resource allocation to squeeze every possible teraflop of processing power out of the burning servers. He drank three more shots of espresso. He stood up and paced the length of the room to keep the blood circulating in his legs, the physical exhaustion settling deep into his joints.


By 3:45 AM, the air in the Tech Hub had grown uncomfortably warm, the ambient temperature pushed into the high eighties by the relentless thermal output of the overworked machines. The smell of ozone was thick and sharp.


Atwater was sitting in his chair, staring blankly at the cascading lines of failed password attempts scrolling endlessly down the fourth monitor, his mind beginning to succumb to the hypnotic, repetitive visual static.


Then, the acoustic frequency of the room changed.


The high-pitched, screaming whine of the cooling fans in the server racks abruptly dropped an octave, spooling down from their maximum redline.


Atwater snapped upright, his spine locking rigid.


He looked at the primary monitor.


The progress bar was gone. The prompt demanding the passkey had vanished.


In the center of the screen, rendered in crisp, high-definition white text against the black background, was a single word: Decrypted.


Atwater let out a breath he didn't realize he had been holding. The digital lock had shattered. He reached out, his hand hovering over the mouse, the sheer weight of the moment demanding a second of absolute stillness. He was about to cross the threshold into the most heavily guarded secrets of a multi-billion-dollar corporate nightmare.


He clicked the mouse.


The isolated virtual sandbox environment exploded with data.


Terabytes of compressed information flooded across the six monitors, rapidly auto-populating into hundreds of nested folders, encrypted PDFs, and massive, sprawling spreadsheet files. The sheer volume of intelligence was staggering. It wasn't just a ledger; it was the entire architectural blueprint of Miriam Daniel’s existence.


Atwater's exhaustion evaporated, completely incinerated by a massive, cold spike of intellectual adrenaline. He was no longer tired. He was a predator who had just found the soft underbelly of his prey.


He began to sort the chaos.


His fingers flew across the keyboard, executing rapid search queries and building temporary indexing algorithms to sift through the noise. He ignored the folders labeled with offshore banking nomenclature—Cayman Islands, Cyprus, Isle of Man. The routing numbers and account balances were astronomical, detailing hundreds of millions of dollars in liquid capital, but they were useless to the Duncans in the short term. They couldn't walk into a bank in Geneva and present an illegal ledger to withdraw funds.


They needed localized, physical leverage. They needed something tangible, something they could hit with bullets and fire.


Atwater refined the search parameters. He excluded international data. He excluded the western seaboard. He filtered the geography down to the Mid-Atlantic region, specifically targeting the logistical overlay for Miriam’s expansion into the Appalachian corridor.


A massive spreadsheet populated on the center screen, titled: Logistics/Transit_Appalachia_Q4.


Atwater leaned in, the glow of the monitor reflecting in his lenses. He began to read the rows of data, his mind processing the acronyms and coded transit designations with terrifying speed.


Miriam Daniel wasn't just moving narcotics through the mountains. She was actively purchasing the infrastructure of the county itself. The spreadsheet detailed a network of newly acquired shell companies buying up bankrupt trucking fleets, dilapidated warehouses, and isolated tracts of land. She was building an unbroken, contiguous supply line that bypassed the major interstate weigh stations entirely.


But purchasing infrastructure required capital. And in the dark corners of the Appalachian underworld, where men like Jimmy the mechanic and Queenie Pearce operated, digital wire transfers left a paper trail that local law enforcement could eventually stumble upon.


They required cash. Physical, untraceable, non-sequential currency.


Atwater’s eyes tracked down the columns until he found a sub-folder labeled Capital Disbursement Schedules.


He opened the file.


The data was recent. It had been updated less than a week before Callie had stolen the drive. It contained the exact scheduling, routing, and volume of the armored transports Miriam used to funnel physical cash into the newly acquired territories.


Atwater cross-referenced the dates with the current calendar. He scrolled past the drops that had already occurred in Kentucky and southern Ohio. He focused entirely on the immediate, localized timeline.


His eyes locked onto a single, highlighted row.


The breath caught in his throat. The mathematical perfection of the discovery was almost beautiful.


There was a massive, localized capital disbursement scheduled to roll directly through their county grid tomorrow morning. Less than twenty-four hours from now.


The ledger detailed the specifics with chilling bureaucratic precision. A heavily armored transit vehicle, disguised as a standard commercial refrigeration truck, was scheduled to move through the county. The payload was listed at five million dollars in physical currency. The destination was a set of GPS coordinates located on the northern ridge.


Queenie Pearce’s territory.


Miriam wasn't just paying Queenie a bounty to hunt Callie. The cartel was actively buying Queenie's loyalty, infusing her localized, ragged operation with enough liquid capital to completely overrun the Duncans' southern hollows. It was a preemptive strike, a massive logistical maneuver designed to crush the Copper Compound by proxy.


But Atwater had the route.


He pulled up a digital topographical map of the county on a secondary monitor, overlaying the GPS waypoints from the cartel ledger onto the rugged terrain.


The armored truck was not taking the main interstate. To maintain a low profile and avoid state trooper patrols, the vehicle was scheduled to navigate the secondary access routes. It was coming up Route 9, winding through the deep valleys before ascending toward the northern ridge.


Atwater traced the glowing red line of the projected route with his cursor. He watched it weave through the contour lines of the mountains, analyzing the geography, looking for the weakness.


He found it instantly.


Ten miles south of Queenie’s territory, Route 9 cut through a massive, naturally occurring geological depression known as the Blackwood Ravine. It was a two-mile stretch of narrow, winding asphalt bordered by sheer, sixty-foot limestone cliffs on both sides. There were no turnoffs. There were no shoulders. Once a vehicle entered the ravine, it was entirely committed to the corridor until it emerged on the other side.


It was a fatal, inescapable chokepoint.


Atwater leaned back in his chair, staring at the map. The defensive posture of the Copper Compound dissolved entirely from his mind. Darell was right. Playing defense against an entity with infinite resources was a slow, agonizing suicide. The only way to survive was to inflict a catastrophic, localized amputation.


If the Duncans intercepted that truck in the Blackwood Ravine, if they slaughtered the cartel transport team and seized the five million dollars in physical capital, they would achieve three immediate, devastating objectives.


First, they would cripple Miriam's ability to fund her expansion into their territory, cutting off the oxygen to her proxy war.


Second, they would publicly humiliate the cartel, proving that the corporate sicarios were vulnerable to the mountain men they sought to eradicate.


Third, and most importantly, they would acquire the capital necessary to fundamentally upgrade their own operational security, buying the high-end tactical assets required to turn the hollow into an impenetrable fortress.


The hunters were about to become the hunted.


Atwater reached across the desk and picked up the heavy black receiver of the internal hardline phone. He punched the two-digit code that connected directly to the master bedroom on the second floor.


It rang once.


"Yeah." Darell’s voice was instantly alert, completely devoid of sleep. The pain of the fresh sutures in his shoulder bled through the single syllable, but the underlying aggression was razor-sharp.


"The cipher is broken," Atwater stated, his voice a crisp, analytical clip that belied the monumental significance of the words. "I have access to the master ledger."


The silence on the line was heavy, charged with sudden, violent potential.


"Bring everyone down to the Hub," Atwater commanded, breaking the protocol of the house. He had never invited the entire crew into his sanctuary. The server room was his isolated domain. But the intelligence he possessed required a unified, strategic council. "Bring Callie. She needs to see this."


"Two minutes," Darell said, and hung up.


Atwater placed the receiver back in the cradle. He stood up, stretching his stiff spine, the joints popping audibly in the quiet room. He walked to the kitchenette, dumped the cold dregs of his espresso into the sink, and turned to face the heavy steel door.


Exactly one hundred and twenty seconds later, the biometric scanner outside chirped, followed by the heavy clack of the deadbolt retracting.


The door swung open.


Darell entered first. He had discarded the bloody, ruined garments from the diner. He wore a clean, dark gray t-shirt, the left sleeve rolled up high to accommodate the thick, white square of sterile gauze taped securely over his stitched deltoid. His face was pale, drawn tight with the localized agony of the wound, but his posture was absolute granite. He moved with the slow, deliberate grace of an apex predator scenting blood.


Blake followed, his chaotic energy barely contained. He was practically vibrating, his mismatched eyes sweeping the interior of the high-tech room with feral curiosity. He carried the customized short-barreled shotgun resting casually across his shoulder, his finger indexed safely along the frame.


Marty entered third, ducking his head slightly to clear the steel doorframe. The enforcer looked grim, a solid wall of muscle and weathered scowls. He didn't look at the flashing monitors; he immediately assumed a tactical position near the entrance, leaning his back against the concrete wall, his arms crossed over his massive chest.


And then, Callie stepped over the threshold.


Atwater studied her intently. She was wearing the oversized gray sweatpants and the fresh, white cotton t-shirt he had provided. Her dirty-blonde hair was dry now, pulled back into a messy, utilitarian knot at the base of her neck. The bruising on her face was stark under the blue light of the servers, and the raw, iodine-stained skin of her hands was clearly visible.


But the fundamental architecture of her presence had completely changed.


When she had first arrived, she had moved like a ghost, shrinking into the walls, minimizing her physical footprint to avoid detection. Now, she walked into the center of the room with her spine straight, her chin level. She did not seek permission to occupy the space. She stepped up beside Darell, her shoulder brushing lightly against his uninjured arm, claiming her position within the vanguard of the syndicate.


She had handed them the launch keys. She belonged in the war room.


"Talk to me, Atwater," Darell commanded, stopping in front of the massive desk, his eyes locked onto the sprawling displays of data.


Atwater did not waste time with preamble. He turned to the keyboard and tapped a single key, shifting the primary focus of the six monitors to the digital topographical map of the county.


"The drive contains the unredacted operational ledgers for Miriam Daniel’s entire logistics network," Atwater began, his voice taking on the cadence of a military briefing. He pointed to the massive spreadsheets filling the peripheral screens. "It is a catastrophic intelligence failure on their part. We possess the routing numbers for her offshore accounts and the identities of her political assets. However, leveraging digital capital requires time we do not currently possess."


"So give me something I can shoot," Blake interrupted, a hungry grin splitting his face.


Atwater hit another key. The red, projected path of the cartel transport vehicle illuminated across the blue topographical lines of the main screen.


"Miriam is attempting to purchase the northern ridge," Atwater explained, looking directly at Callie, acknowledging her intimate understanding of the cartel's methodology. "She is injecting liquid capital into Queenie Pearce’s operation to subsidize a proxy war against us. The ledger confirms a scheduled drop."


He reached out and tapped the screen, indicating a specific waypoint on the red line.


"The day after tomorrow. An armored transport vehicle disguised as a commercial refrigeration truck will enter the county," Atwater stated, the hard facts dropping into the room like heavy stones. "The payload is five million dollars in untraceable, non-sequential hundred-dollar bills. It is a physical cash transfer intended for Queenie’s compound."


Marty pushed off the concrete wall, his heavy boots thudding against the floor. "Five million. That’s a heavy truck. It won't be running with a standard two-man crew. They’ll have a chase vehicle. Heavily armed escorts."


"Correct," Atwater confirmed, nodding to the enforcer. "The ledger mandates a minimum of six corporate sicarios dedicated entirely to the protection of the asset. They will be equipped with fully automatic weaponry and heavy body armor. It is a hardened target."


"Where is the route?" Darell asked, his voice a low, rumbling vibration. He stepped closer to the monitors, his ice-blue eyes analyzing the topographical map, automatically seeking the high ground, the sightlines, the fatal funnels.


"They are avoiding the interstate weigh stations to prevent random cargo inspections," Atwater said, tracing the red line with his finger. "They are taking Route 9 north."


He paused, letting the significance of the geography settle over the room.


"They are routing the transport through the Blackwood Ravine," Atwater finished.


A heavy, predatory silence fell over the Tech Hub. The hum of the server racks seemed to fade into the background.


Every man in the room knew the Blackwood Ravine. It was a treacherous, two-mile scar carved deep into the limestone of the mountain. It was narrow, shaded in perpetual twilight by the towering cliffs, and utterly devoid of cellular reception. Once a vehicle entered the southern mouth of the ravine, it was trapped in a concrete trench until it reached the northern exit.


It was a natural, geographic kill box.


"That's a fatal funnel," Marty observed quietly, a dark, grim satisfaction bleeding into his voice. "You park a heavy rig across the northern exit, you trap them in the trench. They can't turn a commercial truck around in that space. They can't drive up the walls."


"We wire the southern entrance with a localized shape charge," Blake added, his chaotic energy spiking into a lethal, focused joy. He began pacing the small free space behind the desk, racking the shotgun slide in his mind. "The moment the chase vehicle clears the threshold, we blow the rock wall behind them. We seal the back door. They are boxed in. Fish in a barrel."


"The escorts will not surrender," Atwater warned, playing the necessary role of the analytical pessimist. "They are highly trained professionals. They will dismount and attempt to establish a defensive perimeter around the payload."


"Let them dismount," Darell said.


The patriarch of the syndicate turned away from the monitors. He looked at Atwater, then at Blake, then at Marty. The pain in his face was gone, entirely eclipsed by the cold, terrifying certainty of a warlord preparing for a slaughter.


"We don't negotiate with cartel scouts in diners, and we don't let them buy our mountains," Darell stated, his voice ringing with absolute authority. "If Miriam Daniel wants to send five million dollars into my county to fund my own execution, I am going to take her money, and I am going to send her men back to her in pieces."


He turned to Callie.


She was standing perfectly still, her hazel eyes locked onto his. She wasn't trembling. The horrific, violent reality of the plan they were assembling—an ambush that would result in the brutal deaths of at least six men—did not break her. She understood the mathematics of survival better than anyone. It was them, or it was us.


"You gave us the key, little bird," Darell murmured, lifting his uninjured right hand to lightly brush a stray lock of hair behind her ear. "You pointed us at the throat."


"I want her to feel it," Callie whispered, the words carrying a dark, vindictive weight that surprised even Atwater. The battered victim was gone. The survivor had forged herself into a weapon. "I want her to know that her empire is bleeding because of the girl she threw away."


Darell’s lips curved into a slow, devastating smile.


"She'll know," Darell promised. He dropped his hand and turned back to face the room, the tactical briefing transitioning seamlessly into active operational commands.


"Marty," Darell barked, his voice sharp and driving. "Pull the heavy plates from the armory. I want Level IV ceramics for everyone. Prep the heavy, mounted caliber for the bed of the F-250. You and Bruno are going to lock down the northern exit of the ravine."


"Done," Marty grunted, already moving toward the door, his mind calculating the explosive yields required for the ambush.


"Blake," Darell continued, "you're on the southern entrance. You rig the shape charges. When the target clears the mark, you blow the wall and seal them in. Then you provide plunging fire from the ridgeline."


"Oh, it's gonna rain fire, boss," Blake laughed, a dark, joyous sound as he followed Marty out of the room.


Darell turned his focus to the logistics manager. "Atwater. You stay on the digital offensive. I want you to isolate the specific shell accounts associated with this drop. The moment we secure the physical capital in the ravine, I want you to initiate a localized, targeted leak to the federal alphabet agencies. Expose the routing numbers for her shipping ports on the eastern seaboard. Let the feds seize her assets while she's bleeding in the mud."


Atwater nodded sharply, a cold thrill of absolute intellectual dominance rushing through his veins. "I will prepare the data packets. The moment you give the signal, I will burn her infrastructure to the ground."


"We strike in forty-eight hours," Darell declared, the finality of the statement echoing off the cinderblock walls.


The defensive perimeter of the Copper Compound had officially collapsed. The Duncans were no longer hiding in the hollows, waiting for the cartel to find them in the fog.


The hunters had become the hunted. And the Anvil was about to drop.

CHAPTER 17


Forged in Heat


The heavy steel door of the Tech Hub clicked shut behind us, severing the hum of the server racks and sealing Atwater inside his digital war room.


The silence of the subterranean corridor rushed in to fill the void, but the air was no longer cold. It was thick, supercharged with a violent, atmospheric pressure that made the fine hairs on my arms stand on end. The war council was over. The logistical mathematics of our survival had been calculated, the ambush set, the execution ordered. In forty-eight hours, the men walking beside me were going to unleash absolute hellfire in the Blackwood Ravine.


I followed Darell up the wooden stairs. He moved with a slow, deliberate grace, his broad back an impenetrable wall of muscle ahead of me. His left arm hung carefully at his side, the fresh, black nylon sutures Atwater had sewn into his flesh hidden beneath a sterile white gauze pad taped to his shoulder. I watched the microscopic shifts in his latissimus dorsi, tracking the sheer physical power it took for him to mask the pain.


Behind me, the heavy, rhythmic thud of Marty’s steel-toed boots and the lighter, predatory footfalls of Blake boxed me in completely. There was no escape route. There was no lateral movement. I was caught in the center of their formation, caught in the inescapable gravitational pull of their pack.


And for the first time in my life, I didn't want to break the line.


The adrenaline that had been keeping me upright, the frantic, terrified energy that had culminated in smashing my boot against the stone hearth, was mutating. The fear of Miriam Daniel's hit squad had been incinerated the exact second Darell took the encrypted drive from my bloody hand and smiled. I had handed them the launch keys to a corporate apocalypse, and the resulting high was a dark, intoxicating drug flooding my bloodstream.


We reached the top of the stairs, stepping into the dim, moonlit expanse of the living room. Darell didn't stop. He didn't turn toward the kitchen or the front door. He bypassed the main floor entirely, his boots silent on the Persian rugs as he headed for the wide, sweeping mahogany staircase that led to the second floor.


He was taking me to his territory.


I climbed the stairs, my bare feet sinking into the plush runner. The second floor of the compound was an area I had barely seen, a sanctuary reserved exclusively for the men who ruled the mountain. The air up here smelled intensely of seasoned cedar, old leather, and the sharp, clean bite of winter draft slipping through the microscopic gaps in the reinforced window frames.


Darell reached the end of the long hallway. He pushed open a massive, solid oak double door.


He stepped inside and stopped, turning slowly to face the corridor.

CHAPTER 18


The Queen's Move


The dusty, anemic morning sunlight struggled to penetrate the heavy layer of industrial grime coating the high skylights of the corrugated metal warehouse. What little illumination managed to filter through the filth fell in sharp, slanted columns, illuminating millions of agitated dust motes dancing in the stagnant air.


Queenie Pearce sat behind a massive, incredibly tacky desk constructed of imitation mahogany and cheap gold-painted trim. She inhaled deeply, the paper of her Virginia Slim cigarette crackling softly as the cherry burned bright orange. She held the smoke in her lungs, savoring the harsh, acrid bite of the cheap tobacco, before exhaling a long, thin, gray plume toward the water-stained ceiling tiles.


Her office was a monument to unearned arrogance. It was perched on a raised steel mezzanine overlooking the sprawling, chaotic floor of her primary methamphetamine packaging facility. The walls of the office were lined with faux-velvet wallpaper in a garish shade of burgundy. A massive, ostentatious crystal chandelier—entirely out of place in a corrugated steel building—hung directly over her head, though half the bulbs were burned out.


The air in the room was dense, a suffocating cocktail of conflicting odors. The stale, lingering stench of decades of indoor smoking warred with the heavy, cloying scent of her signature cheap floral perfume. But beneath the tobacco and the artificial gardenia, the foundational smell of the northern ridge was inescapable. It was the sharp, highly volatile chemical tang of anhydrous ammonia, lithium, and ether rising from the cook stations below. It was the smell of poison. It was the smell of her empire.


Queenie lifted her left hand to tap the long ash of her cigarette into a heavy glass ashtray overflowing with crushed butts. The movement sent a cacophonous jingle echoing through the room. She wore no less than eight heavy gold rings, her knuckles swollen and stiff with early-onset arthritis, her wrists laden with thick, braided gold bracelets that clattered against the faux-mahogany desk. She was a woman who believed that wealth required audible, constant demonstration.


At fifty-five, Queenie retained the hard, brittle remnants of a mountain beauty that had long since been calcified by greed and harsh chemicals. Her hair was dyed an aggressive, flat shade of raven black, teased and sprayed into a high, rigid helmet. Her makeup was applied with a heavy hand—thick black eyeliner, dramatic rouge, and lips painted a glossy, bleeding-heart red.


She stared down at the ledger resting on her blotter.


Unlike the meticulous, heavily encrypted digital spreadsheets Atwater Briggs maintained in the sterile vacuum of the Duncan’s subterranean server room, Queenie’s ledger was a physical, spiral-bound notebook. The pages were dog-eared, stained with coffee rings and questionable chemical smears. The columns of numbers were scrawled in erratic, barely legible blue ink.


The numbers were trending in the wrong direction.

Her drivers were careless addicts, her cooks were sloppy, and her territory was financially stagnating.


She controlled the northern ridge of the county. It was a sprawling, impoverished expanse of dying coal towns and rusted trailer parks. It was a captive market, but it was a poor one.

They didn't cook their product in rusted propane tanks in the woods; they distilled artisanal, high-proof ethanol in massive, gleaming copper columns hidden in the bedrock. They didn't sell to the desperate locals; they supplied high-end syndicates across state lines.


Darell Duncan. The patriarch. He sat in his fortified, cedar-scented cabin, surrounded by his feral brothers, looking down his patrician nose at her operation. The Duncans considered themselves righteous criminals. They followed a code. They protected their borders. She hated them with a pure, unadulterated passion that burned hotter than the ether on her factory floor. She wanted the southern hollows. She wanted the copper stills. She wanted to watch Darell Duncan bleed out in the mud, stripped of his arrogant, untouchable aura.


But wanting a kingdom and possessing the army required to take it were two entirely different realities. Every time Queenie had attempted to probe the southern borders, her men had disappeared. The Duncans didn't issue warnings. They issued localized executions.


A sharp, heavy knock on the frosted glass of her office door pulled Queenie from her bitter reverie.


"Yeah. Come on in, Bo," Queenie drawled, her voice a thick, syrupy Appalachian rasp that dripped with condescension.


The door pushed open. Bo, one of her primary enforcers, stepped into the office. He was a large, unkempt man wearing a stained denim jacket over a faded band t-shirt. His eyes were perpetually bloodshot, his jaw working a piece of chewing gum with frantic, chemical-induced energy.


He didn't come in alone.


Standing slightly behind him, looking entirely out of place in the grimy, chemical-soaked environment of the mezzanine, was a woman.


Queenie crushed her Virginia Slim into the ashtray, her heavily mascaraed eyes locking onto the stranger.


The woman was small, petite, and dressed with an understated, expensive elegance that instantly triggered Queenie's localized class resentment. She wore a soft beige cashmere sweater that likely cost more than Bo's entire wardrobe, paired with tailored charcoal trousers and immaculate designer flats. Her mousy brown hair was pulled back into a severe, nervous bun. She clutched a sleek leather portfolio tightly to her chest, her knuckles white.


The woman was trembling. It was a subtle vibration, barely visible, but Queenie was a predator who had spent her entire life reading the weakness in others. She smelled the fear. It radiated off the woman like cheap cologne.


"Found her sitting in a rented Lincoln Continental at the bottom of the access road," Bo grunted, gesturing with a thick, grease-stained thumb toward the stranger. "Said she needed to talk to the boss. Said she had a business proposition. I patted her down. No wire. No weapons. Just a whole lot of shaking."


Queenie leaned back in her faux-leather executive chair. It squeaked loudly in protest. She laced her heavily jeweled fingers together, resting them over her stomach. She let the silence stretch, a deliberate, psychological tactic designed to maximize the discomfort of the outsider.


She looked the woman up and down, offering a slow, derisive smile that didn't reach her eyes.


"Well, sugar," Queenie finally spoke, her tone dripping with mock sweetness. "You look like you took a wrong turn off the interstate on your way to a country club brunch. You lost?"


The woman swallowed hard. She took a single, hesitant step forward, moving away from Bo's looming presence.


"No," the woman said. Her voice was slightly breathless, pitched high with anxiety. "I am exactly where I intended to be. Are you Queenie Pearce?"


"I am the queen of this ridge, honey," Queenie corrected, emphasizing the title. "And I don't generally entertain walk-ins. Especially not nervous little city mice who roll up to my gates uninvited. Bo here should have put a bullet in your engine block and left you for the coyotes."


"I have money," the woman blurted out, the words tumbling over each other in a rush. She clutched the leather portfolio tighter against her cashmere sweater. "I have a substantial amount of capital, and I require a specific... service. A service I was informed your organization specializes in providing."


Queenie raised a perfectly drawn, artificial eyebrow. Money was the universal solvent. It washed away trespassing violations and erased the rigid boundaries of paranoia.


"Bo," Queenie said, not taking her eyes off the trembling woman. "Wait outside. Close the door."


Bo grunted an acknowledgment, turning his heavy frame and stepping out onto the catwalk. He pulled the frosted glass door shut, leaving the two women alone in the dense, perfumed air of the office.


Queenie gestured lazily toward a cheap, plastic folding chair positioned across from her desk. "Take a seat, sugar. Tell me your name. And then tell me exactly how much capital we're discussing before I lose my patience and have Bo throw you off the catwalk."


The woman walked to the chair. She didn't sit immediately. She looked at the plastic seat, then at the stained carpet, then finally at Queenie.


And then, the most terrifying, fundamental shift occurred.


It did not happen slowly. It happened in the span of a single, drawn breath.


The woman’s shoulders, which had been drawn inward in a posture of protective submission, suddenly snapped backward, aligning into a rigid, immaculate posture. The frantic, nervous trembling in her hands ceased instantly, her fingers relaxing their death grip on the leather portfolio. The breathless, high-pitched anxiety in her voice evaporated.


When she looked at Queenie again, the wide, frightened doe eyes were gone. They were replaced by a gaze so flat, so cold, and so utterly devoid of human empathy that Queenie felt a sudden, involuntary chill slide down her spine.


The mask of the terrified victim vanished, revealing the absolute, calculated sociopathy resting beneath the surface.


"My name is Genevieve Hersey," the woman stated.


Her voice had dropped an octave. It was crisp, devoid of any regional accent, and entirely stripped of the casual contractions that defined informal speech. It was the voice of a corporate executioner.


Genevieve did not sit in the plastic chair. She stepped up to the edge of the faux-mahogany desk and placed her leather portfolio flat on the surface, directly over Queenie’s messy ledger.


"And I do not require a service, Ms. Pearce," Genevieve continued, her tone holding the undeniable ring of absolute authority. "I am here to offer you an acquisition. I represent Miriam Daniel."


Queenie’s breath caught in her throat. The heavy gold bracelets clattered against the desk as she instinctively pulled her hands back.


Miriam Daniel.


The name was a dark, bloody myth whispered across the interstate supply lines. The cartel queen who had recently moved into the Mid-Atlantic, swallowing up independent operations with ruthless, corporate efficiency. Queenie had heard the rumors. Everyone in the Appalachian underworld had heard the rumors.


And Queenie had definitely heard the frantic, scrambled radio chatter regarding the absolute slaughter that had occurred at the Crossroads Diner yesterday afternoon. Three men in tailored suits, gunned down in twelve seconds by Darell Duncan. The county was practically vibrating with the impending fallout.


"The cartel," Queenie breathed, the syrupy drawl faltering for a fraction of a second. She looked at the woman standing before her, re-evaluating the threat matrix. This wasn't a victim. This was a proxy. A razor-blade wrapped in cashmere.


"My employer is currently expanding her logistical infrastructure," Genevieve said, her hands resting lightly on the portfolio. "However, we have encountered a localized complication within this sector. A complication that requires immediate, decisive eradication. We are aware of the geopolitical boundaries of this county. We are aware of your... stagnation."


Queenie’s eyes narrowed, her pride instantly flaring up to mask her sudden apprehension. "Watch your mouth, city girl. You might represent heavy money, but you are standing in my house. I don't stagnate. I hold the line."


"You hold a fraction of the market," Genevieve corrected smoothly, entirely unbothered by the threat. "You control the northern ridge. The southern hollows belong to the Duncan family. They possess the high-end distillation infrastructure. They possess the favorable terrain. And, according to our intelligence, they possess the loyalty of the local law enforcement. You are trapped in a geographic and economic cul-de-sac, Ms. Pearce."


Queenie ground her teeth together. The clinical, detached recitation of her own failures stung worse than an insult. "What the hell do you want?"


"A high-value asset belonging to Ms. Daniel has absconded," Genevieve explained, her voice as smooth as polished glass. "A young woman. Dirty-blonde hair. She is currently carrying a proprietary digital ledger that belongs to the organization. Two days ago, she crashed a vehicle near the southern perimeter of the county."


"The Audi," Queenie murmured, connecting the dots. Her own scouts had reported the wrecked car before the Sheriff’s deputies had locked down the ravine.


"She did not die in the crash," Genevieve confirmed. "Our tracking data indicates she was acquired by the local element. She is currently being harbored by Darell Duncan within the confines of his fortified compound."


Queenie let out a harsh, barking laugh. The sound was genuine, fueled by the sheer absurdity of the situation. She reached for her pack of Virginia Slims, tapping a fresh cigarette against her gold-ringed thumb.


"Well, ain't that a tragedy," Queenie mocked, bringing the lighter to the tip of the cigarette. She inhaled, letting the smoke curl out of her nostrils. "If Darell has your girl behind his blast doors, she’s gone. You cartel folks might have numbers in the city, but you don't know the hollows. The Duncans have that property wired to blow. You try a frontal assault on their perimeter, your men are gonna come back in trash bags. Darell Duncan doesn't give up what he claims."


"We are fully aware of their defensive capabilities," Genevieve said. She opened the leather portfolio. Inside was a single, pristine map of the county, marked with specific tactical overlays. "A frontal assault on a fortified position is mathematically inefficient. It results in unacceptable personnel losses and risks the destruction of the asset we seek to retrieve. We do not intend to breach their compound while it is fully occupied."


Queenie stopped smoking. She looked at the map, then back up at the cold, calculating eyes of the accountant.


"We intend to draw them out," Genevieve stated. "We intend to separate the Duncan men from their fortress, isolate them in a geographically disadvantageous location, and eliminate them. While they are distracted, a secondary retrieval team will extract our property from the undefended compound."


"Draw Darell Duncan out?" Queenie scoffed, shaking her head. "You're delusional. Darell doesn't leave the mountain unless he has a reason. He's paranoid. He's locked down tight right now because your boys shot up his diner yesterday. He ain't coming out to play."


"He will leave," Genevieve countered with absolute, terrifying certainty. "Because we are going to offer him an irresistible target."


Genevieve reached into her pocket and produced a silver pen. She used the tip to point to a specific coordinate on the map.


"Ms. Daniel is scheduling a massive injection of physical capital into the region," Genevieve explained. "Five million dollars in untraceable currency, transported in a disguised commercial refrigeration truck. The official destination for this drop is listed as a warehouse under your operational control."


Queenie’s breath hitched. Five million dollars. The sheer magnitude of the number echoed in her skull. It was enough money to buy the entire county twice over. It was enough to arm an army.


"Are you offering me five million dollars to hit the Duncans?" Queenie asked, her greed instantly surging to the forefront, overriding her caution.


"No," Genevieve said bluntly, instantly shattering the fantasy. "The five million dollars is bait."


Queenie scowled, leaning forward over the desk. "Bait for what?"


"For Darell," Genevieve said. "We allowed the Duncans to acquire the digital logistics ledger detailing this specific transport route. We wanted them to know the money was coming. We wanted them to know it was intended for you."


Queenie stared at the accountant, the layers of the manipulation slowly unspooling in her mind. It was a staggering display of psychological warfare.


"You fed them the route," Queenie whispered.


"Darell Duncan is a tactical thinker," Genevieve analyzed, speaking of the warlord as if he were merely an algorithm to be solved. "He knows he cannot survive a protracted siege against our organization. He will calculate that his only chance of survival is a preemptive, crippling strike. If he believes he can intercept five million dollars meant to fund his rival—you—he will not be able to resist the opportunity. He will attempt to hijack the transport."


"He'll hit the truck," Queenie realized, her eyes locked on the map. She followed the red line Genevieve had drawn. "He'll hit it here. In the Blackwood Ravine. It's the only place on the route where the truck can't turn around."


"Precisely," Genevieve nodded, acknowledging Queenie's localized geographic knowledge. "The Duncans will stage an ambush in the ravine tomorrow morning. They will bring their heavy ordnance. They will commit their primary personnel to the strike."


"And when they do?" Queenie asked, her heart hammering a frantic rhythm against her ribs.


"When they execute the ambush, they will find the transport truck completely empty," Genevieve said, her voice dropping into a chilling, absolute register. "And they will find themselves trapped in the ravine, surrounded by a fully equipped, heavily armed cartel assault team holding the high ground."


The trap was perfect. It was a masterclass in exploiting an enemy's own aggressive instincts against them. Miriam Daniel wasn't going to fight the Duncans on their terms; she was forcing them to fight on hers.


"Where do I come in?" Queenie asked. She wasn't a fool. The cartel didn't need her permission to drive a truck into the ravine. If Genevieve was sitting in her office, it meant Queenie was required to make the trap snap shut.


"The Duncans know the terrain better than our operatives," Genevieve stated. "If the ambush fails, they will utilize secondary, hidden egress routes to escape the ravine. Our men do not know the topography well enough to seal the perimeter completely."


Genevieve tapped the silver pen against the faux-mahogany desk.


"We require an anvil," Genevieve said. "My employer will provide the hammer. We will station our primary assault team inside the ravine. We require your organization to secure the ridgelines above the chokepoint. We need your men to provide plunging fire and seal the exit routes. We require a joint operational force to ensure total annihilation."


Queenie leaned back, taking a long, slow drag of her cigarette. The ash fell from the tip, scattering across the dirty carpet.


She looked at the map. She looked at the cold, sociopathic woman standing in front of her.


This was the moment. This was the defining pivot point of her entire criminal existence. If she allied with the Duncans, she remained a second-tier meth cook scraping by on the northern ridge. If she allied with Miriam Daniel, she was entering a partnership with a great white shark.


"You want my boys to stand on the ridge and shoot Darell Duncan like a fish in a barrel," Queenie summarized, a slow, venomous smile spreading across her red-painted lips.


"Yes," Genevieve said.


"And what is my compensation for providing the anvil?" Queenie demanded. She wasn't going to bleed her own men for a cartel without a guaranteed, massive return on investment. "The five million is fake. You already said it's bait."


"Your compensation is the southern hollows," Genevieve offered, the terms of the treaty laid bare. "When the Duncans are eliminated, Ms. Daniel has no interest in maintaining a permanent presence in this county. Her only objective is the retrieval of the asset and the digital ledger. Once our property is secured, the cartel forces will withdraw entirely."


Genevieve closed the portfolio, sliding it slightly toward Queenie.


"The Copper Compound, the subterranean distillation infrastructure, the established supply routes—Ms. Daniel will cede complete operational control of the entire territory to you, Ms. Pearce," Genevieve promised. "You will possess a monopoly. You will upgrade from manufacturing low-grade methamphetamine to controlling a multi-million-dollar artisanal ethanol syndicate. You will be the sole authority in the region."


Queenie’s mind raced. The southern hollows. The copper stills. The absolute destruction of the arrogant, self-righteous Duncan family. It was everything she had coveted for a decade, wrapped in a cartel-subsidized bow.


"Darell isn't stupid," Queenie countered, playing devil's advocate, testing the structural integrity of the plan. "If he leaves the compound to hit the truck, he's going to leave a guard behind to protect your girl. And he's going to wire the access roads. Your secondary team won't be able to just walk up to the front door and knock."


"We are aware of the perimeter defenses," Genevieve said patiently. "That is why the secondary retrieval team requires the exact, localized GPS coordinates of the specific structure she is housed in. The compound is vast. We cannot waste time breaching decoys while the primary battle is occurring in the ravine."


"And how do you plan to get the exact coordinates?" Queenie asked, crossing her arms. "The Duncans run localized signal jammers. You can't just track her from a satellite."


A slow, chilling smile touched the corners of Genevieve’s mouth. It was the first display of emotion the woman had shown since dropping her nervous facade, and it was deeply unsettling.


Genevieve reached into the pocket of her tailored trousers. She pulled out a small, sleek smartphone and set it on the desk next to the map.


"Callie is currently in possession of a burner phone," Genevieve explained. "The device is programmed to route its signal through a heavily encrypted cartel proxy server the moment it connects to a cellular tower. The Duncans' jammers block incoming signals, but they cannot entirely suppress an outbound, localized ping if the device is activated near an exterior wall."


"She knows you're hunting her," Queenie pointed out. "Why the hell would she turn a phone on?"


"Because she does not know I am hunting her," Genevieve corrected softly. The sheer, calculated malice in her voice was a physical weight in the room. "Callie believes she stole the drive entirely on her own. She believes I am an innocent bystander, an accountant trapped in Miriam's office."


Genevieve tapped the screen of the smartphone, waking it up.


"Before she escaped, I staged a highly convincing, panicked voicemail on her secure line," Genevieve continued, outlining the psychological trap with the clinical detachment of a scientist dissecting an insect. "I informed her that Miriam had discovered the theft. I informed her that I was in imminent, mortal danger because of her actions. I begged her to call me so I could escape."


Queenie stared at the phone. The realization of what Genevieve had done sent a ripple of dark, genuine respect through the older woman.


"You played her," Queenie whispered.


"Callie's primary psychological vulnerability is her empathy," Genevieve stated. "She cannot tolerate the concept of another person suffering for her survival. The Duncans may offer her physical protection, but they cannot assuage her guilt. The pressure is building. She will reach a breaking point. She will attempt to save me."


Genevieve looked down at the dark screen of the phone.


"She will turn the burner phone on. She will step near a window to find a signal. And she will call this number," Genevieve said. "When she does, our servers will instantly triangulate her exact location within a three-meter radius, bypassing the Duncans' localized jammers. We will have the precise coordinates of the structure she is occupying."


Queenie slowly nodded, the sheer brilliance of the manipulation settling over her. The Duncans were preparing for a physical siege, entirely unaware that the fatal breach was already resting inside the pocket of the woman they were protecting.


"And once you have the coordinates?" Queenie asked.


"We wait for the Duncans to deploy to the Blackwood Ravine," Genevieve said. "The moment they engage the empty transport truck, your men will seal the exits. Simultaneously, our extraction team will execute a localized, overwhelming strike on the specific coordinates provided by the phone ping. They will breach the structure, retrieve Callie and the ledger, and burn the remainder of the compound to the ground to cover their tracks."


Genevieve folded her hands in front of her, the presentation complete.


"The Duncans die in the ravine. The asset is returned. You inherit the ashes of their empire," Genevieve summarized. "Do we have an alliance, Ms. Pearce?"


Queenie looked around her dingy, faux-velvet office. She smelled the ether. She thought about the pristine, gleaming copper towers hidden in the southern bedrock, generating millions of dollars in clean, quiet capital. She thought about Darell Duncan’s arrogant, icy stare.


She reached forward and picked up her heavy gold lighter. She flipped the lid open with a sharp clink, striking the flint, and brought the flame to a fresh Virginia Slim. She inhaled deeply, the toxic smoke filling her lungs.


She blew the smoke directly across the desk, the gray cloud washing over Genevieve’s immaculate cashmere sweater. Genevieve did not blink. She did not cough. She simply waited.


"You tell Miriam Daniel she has her anvil," Queenie Pearce declared, her syrupy drawl hardening into a venomous, absolute vow. "I'll put twenty of my best shooters on the ridges above the Blackwood Ravine tomorrow morning. When Darell drives his armored truck into that trench, we’re going to bury him under a mountain of lead."


"Excellent," Genevieve said. She did not offer her hand to shake. The transaction was purely mechanical.


Genevieve picked up the leather portfolio, sliding the map back inside. She reached into her pocket and produced a secondary device—a ruggedized digital tracking tablet—and placed it on the desk.


"Monitor this screen," Genevieve instructed, sliding the tablet toward the meth boss. "The moment Callie calls my secondary line, I will answer from my mobile command unit and keep her engaged. Our proxy server will route the finalized GPS triangulation data directly to this tablet in real-time. You will have the exact coordinates to position your perimeter blockers."


"I'll be watching, sugar," Queenie sneered, eyeing the tablet like a loaded weapon.


"Once the lock is confirmed, our extraction teams move in," Genevieve finished. She turned away from the desk, her posture perfect, her movements precise.


She walked toward the frosted glass door, her expensive flats clicking softly against the stained carpet. She opened the door, stepping out onto the catwalk where Bo was waiting. She did not look back.


Queenie sat alone in her office, the silence heavy and charged with the impending violence.


She looked down at the messy, chaotic ledger of her failing meth empire. She closed the spiral notebook and pushed it to the far corner of the desk.


She pulled the sleek, modern tracking tablet toward her, resting her heavily jeweled hands on either side of the black plastic casing.


The trap was set. The jaws were open, lined with the combined lethality of a corporate cartel and a desperate mountain syndicate. The Duncans were sharpening their knives, entirely oblivious to the fact that they were preparing to charge headlong into a slaughterhouse.


Queenie took a long, satisfying drag of her cigarette, her dark eyes fixed on the blank screen of the tablet, waiting with absolute, venomous anticipation for the little bird in the southern hollows to sing.
