c31N



CHAPTER 27


Unleashing the Hounds


The slaughter in the lower valley had been brief, brutally efficient, and entirely unfulfilling. Darell and the pack had successfully intercepted the cartel’s secondary strike team as the operatives attempted to navigate the access roads through the freezing rain. The Duncans had descended upon them like wolves, utilizing thermal optics to systematically execute the corporate sicarios in the dark before the mercenaries even had a chance to return fire.


But the adrenaline of the victory had instantly turned to ash when Atwater, monitoring the scrambled frequencies from the mobile command center, intercepted a terrifying, automated radio confirmation bouncing from a localized cartel proxy server: Primary Target Secured. Incendiaries Deployed.


They hadn't just been fighting the extraction force; they had been deliberately drawn away from the hollow by a perfectly executed diversion.


The stench of the destruction hit Blake Stokes a full mile before the crippled Ford F-250 finally crested the southern ridge on their frantic, desperate race back home. It wasn't the clean, familiar scent of seasoned hickory burning in the massive stone hearth. It was the toxic, suffocating odor of melted copper, vaporized electronics, and incinerated wood.


When Darell slammed the ruined truck into park near the tree line, the horrific reality of the devastation was illuminated by the pale, mocking light of the Appalachian moon.


The Copper Compound was gone.


The sprawling cedar cabin, the physical manifestation of three generations of Duncan legacy, had been reduced to a smoldering, blackened footprint of ash and jagged, smoking timbers. The structural integrity of the roof had collapsed entirely, crushing the interior beneath tons of burning debris. But the true horror lay beneath the foundation. The massive, hidden ventilation shafts leading down to the subterranean Hollow had functioned as exhaust pipes for a localized thermobaric explosion. The earth around the foundation was buckled and cracked, and the surviving grates were currently vomiting a steady stream of thick, black smoke into the freezing, snow-filled sky.


Blake didn't wait for Darell to issue an order. He didn't wait for the engine to die. He threw the heavy, armored passenger door open and hit the muddy, slush-covered ground at a full sprint.


"Callie!" Blake roared.


The sound tore from his throat, a raw, feral, agonizing scream that echoed off the surrounding cliffs and died instantly in the smoke. He crashed through the tree line, his heavy boots sinking deep into the freezing mud where the heat of the inferno had melted the snowpack.


"Callie!" he screamed again, his voice cracking, entirely stripped of its usual chaotic, vibrating amusement.


He reached the edge of what used to be the front porch. The wooden stairs were gone, leaving only the charred, smoking cinderblock foundation. The heat radiating from the dying embers was still intense enough to singe the fine hairs on his arms and forearms, but Blake didn't stop. He scrambled up onto the hot concrete, the thick rubber soles of his boots smoking as they made contact.


He pulled the heavy hunting knife from the sheath on his belt, an entirely useless weapon against the fire, but his hands needed to hold onto something lethal to keep his mind from fracturing completely.


"She's not in the fire," a low, gravelly voice rumbled behind him.


Blake spun around, his mismatched eyes wide, bloodshot, and completely unhinged. The adrenaline coursing through his veins was a toxic, corrosive acid, demanding a physical outlet before it dissolved his sanity.


Bruno Harding stood at the edge of the foundation, his massive frame completely covered in a fresh layer of soot and mud from his grueling trek back from the northern ridges. The tracker wasn't looking at the burning ruins of the house. His dark, observant eyes were locked onto the ground, scanning the muddy perimeter.


"How the hell do you know that?" Blake snarled, the blade of his knife trembling as he pointed it toward the smoking crater where the living room used to be. "They blew the vents! They dropped the roof!"


Darell appeared beside Bruno, his face a terrifying mask of absolute, glacial detachment. The blood from his sutured shoulder had soaked entirely through his dark gray henley, drying into a stiff, rust-colored stain. Atwater and Marty flanked him, the enforcer still clutching his cracked sternum, the logistics manager staring blankly at the destroyed ventilation shafts where his multimillion-dollar digital infrastructure had been incinerated.


"Bruno is right," Darell stated, his voice a low, vibrating hum that barely cleared the sound of the crackling embers. "Look at the ground, Blake."


Blake tore his eyes away from the fire, forcing his frantic, hyper-aroused brain to process the physical environment.


The snow surrounding the house had melted into a wide radius of thick, viscous mud. And the mud was heavily churned.


"Multiple footfalls," Bruno analyzed, stepping forward, his heavy boots carefully avoiding the established tracks. "Heavy tactical treads. Minimum of a six-man extraction team. They approached from the eastern tree line. They breached the front doors with a localized shaped charge."


Bruno stopped near a patch of mud that was significantly more disturbed than the rest, located roughly where the kitchen archway used to stand.


"There was a struggle here," Bruno continued, crouching down, his large, gloved fingers hovering over a deep, dragging impression in the dirt. "Two sets of tactical boots bracketing a smaller, bare footprint. The stride is erratic. She was resisting."


Blake scrambled down from the hot foundation, his chest heaving, his lungs burning as he sucked in the toxic, smoke-filled air. He crouched beside Bruno, staring at the small, desperate footprint.


"She fought them," Blake whispered, the chaotic fury in his chest momentarily eclipsed by a profound, agonizing surge of protective pride. "She fought them barefoot."


"They dragged her out," Bruno confirmed, tracing the dragging marks leading away from the house and toward the main driveway. "They didn't execute her inside the structure. Miriam Daniel requires the ledger, and she requires the password to unlock it. She couldn't leave Callie in the fire."


"Where did they take her?" Marty demanded, his deep voice scraping against the quiet.


"The tracks lead back to the primary access road," Bruno said, standing up. "They utilized heavy, four-wheel-drive vehicles. The tire treads match the armored Suburban we engaged in the ravine. They retreated north."


"North," Darell repeated, the single syllable dripping with absolute, homicidal intent.


"Queenie Pearce’s territory," Atwater stated, stepping forward, his wire-rimmed glasses reflecting the dying embers. The logistics manager's analytical mind had already reconstructed the tactical timeline. "Genevieve Hersey orchestrated the trap. Queenie Pearce provided the anvil in the ravine. It is a joint operational force. The cartel extraction team wouldn't attempt to navigate the icy highways out of the state immediately. They would fall back to a secure, localized staging area to interrogate the asset and finalize the territorial transfer."


"Queenie’s warehouse," Blake snarled, the pieces finally snapping together in his frantic mind.


He stood up, his grip tightening on the handle of his hunting knife until his knuckles turned stark white. The sheer, overwhelming panic that Callie was dead had evaporated, replaced entirely by a cold, singular, apocalyptic rage. They had taken her. They had ripped her out of his home, bound her, and dragged her back to the monster she had spent two years running from.


Blake looked at Darell.


The patriarch of the Duncan syndicate was standing perfectly still, staring at the smoking ruins of his empire. Three generations of illicit, blood-soaked capital, thousands of gallons of high-proof ethanol, the air-gapped servers, the beautiful mahogany floors where his brothers had bled to protect their own, and the sprawling river-stone hearth where he had finally watched the terrified, hunted look vanish from Callie's beautiful eyes—all of it was gone, incinerated in a single, catastrophic blast.


"Darell," Blake said, his voice dropping an octave, losing the chaotic edge, settling into the cold, hard frequency of a syndicate enforcer requesting permission to execute. "Give the order."


Darell slowly turned his head. His ice-blue eyes were completely black, the pupils blown wide with a terrifying, absolute absence of humanity. He was no longer a man. He was a localized extinction event waiting to be unleashed.


"We are no longer defending a perimeter," Darell declared, his voice ringing with a brutal, unyielding finality that echoed off the surrounding cliffs. "We are no longer hiding in the hollows. Miriam Daniel wants to purchase the Appalachian mountains with blood. I am going to drown her in it."


He didn't look at the burning house again. He turned on his heel and walked with heavy, deliberate strides toward the massive, red-painted structure of the main barn that sat fifty yards away from the primary residence.


The barn had survived the thermobaric blast, shielded by the distance and the dense thicket of pines separating it from the house.


The four brothers followed Darell without a single word of hesitation. The synergetic, telepathic connection of the pack was fully engaged. They were moving as a single, lethal organism.


Darell reached the heavy sliding doors of the barn and threw them open. The interior smelled of old hay, diesel fuel, and heavy machinery. He bypassed the two customized, heavily armored Ford F-250s parked in the center bay—the backup vehicles Atwater had pre-staged weeks ago.


He walked to the far back corner of the barn, stopping in front of a completely unremarkable, concrete-poured floor drain.


Darell crouched down. He ignored the pain in his left shoulder, gripping the heavy iron grate covering the drain with his right hand. He hauled it upward, tossing the heavy metal aside. He reached into the dark hole, his fingers finding a concealed, biometric keypad recessed into the concrete.


He pressed his thumb against the scanner.


A heavy, hydraulic hiss echoed beneath the concrete floor. A massive, four-by-four-foot square of the barn floor suddenly shifted, dropping an inch before sliding smoothly to the right, revealing a steep flight of industrial steel stairs leading down into the darkness.


"The primary armory was in the house," Atwater noted quietly, standing at the edge of the opening.


"The primary armory was for defense," Darell corrected, stepping onto the first steel stair. "This is for eradication."


Blake followed him down into the subterranean bunker.


The space was small, claustrophobic, and entirely devoid of the comforts of the house. The walls were raw, unpainted concrete. A single fluorescent bulb hummed overhead, casting a harsh, sterile light over the terrifying arsenal bolted to the walls.


This was not a collection of hunting rifles and customized handguns. This was military-grade, suppressed, fully automatic ordnance.


"Strip the compromised plates," Darell ordered, already pulling his blood-stained henley over his head, exposing the stark white bandages on his shoulder. "Load fresh ceramics. Heavy carriers. We are not engaging in a tactical retreat. We are pushing through the fatal funnel."


Marty didn't argue. He unbuckled his ruined tactical vest, dropping the cracked Level IV plate onto the concrete floor with a heavy thud. He pulled a pristine, heavy-duty carrier from the wall rack, adjusting the straps over his massive chest, ignoring the sharp spike of pain from his fractured sternum.


Blake moved to the far wall. The chaotic, vibrating energy had returned, but it was entirely focused, entirely lethal. He bypassed the standard M4 carbines and the suppressed submachine guns.


He reached out and grabbed a massive, heavily customized weapon from the rack. It was an Atchisson AA-12, a fully automatic, 12-gauge combat shotgun capable of firing three hundred rounds per minute. It was a weapon designed specifically for close-quarters devastation. He snatched three heavy, twenty-round drum magazines loaded with 00 buckshot and slammed them into the deep pouches of his tactical vest.


"I'm taking the point," Blake stated, his mismatched eyes blazing as he racked the heavy bolt of the weapon.


"You breach the doors," Darell agreed, pulling a heavy, black ballistic vest over his bare torso, tightening the straps with his uninjured arm. He grabbed a suppressed, short-barreled Heckler & Koch HK416 assault rifle, securing it to a single-point sling across his chest. "Marty, you hold the perimeter. Suppress any external reinforcements. Bruno, you take the high ground. Eliminate the snipers."


"And Atwater?" Bruno asked, pulling a heavy, bolt-action .338 Lapua Magnum sniper rifle from the rack, securing a massive, thermal-imaging optic to the top rail.


"I am overriding the digital security grid of Queenie’s warehouse," Atwater said, grabbing a ruggedized military laptop and shoving it into a tactical backpack. He pulled a suppressed SIG MPX submachine gun from the rack, checking the chamber with clinical precision. "I will isolate their localized comms and kill the exterior lighting. They will fight in the dark."


Darell strapped his customized Colt .45 into a drop-leg holster strapped to his right thigh. He grabbed three fragmentation grenades from a heavy metal crate, clipping them securely to the webbing of his vest.


He looked at his brothers. They were fully militarized. They were covered in soot, sweat, and blood, heavily armored and carrying enough firepower to level a city block. The moral code that usually governed their violence—the strict adherence to localized defense and isolation—had been completely incinerated in the ashes of the compound.


There were no rules of engagement tonight. There was no quarter.


"They took her out of our house," Darell said, his voice a low, terrifying rumble that vibrated through the concrete bunker. "They put their hands on her. They dragged her back to the cage."


He racked the charging handle of the HK416, the mechanical clack echoing sharply.


"We are going to Queenie Pearce's warehouse," Darell commanded, his ice-blue eyes locking onto each of his brothers in turn, transmitting the absolute, homicidal finality of the order. "We are going to kill the guards. We are going to kill the sicarios. We are going to kill Queenie. If Miriam Daniel is in that building, we are going to tear her apart."


"No prisoners," Blake agreed, a dark, feral smile splitting his face.


"No survivors," Marty rumbled.


Darell turned and ascended the steel stairs. The pack followed, a heavily armed column of absolute destruction rising from the subterranean bunker and stepping back out into the freezing, smoke-filled air of the barn.


They moved with rapid, synchronized efficiency.


Darell vaulted into the driver's seat of the heaviest backup armored F-250, the suspension groaning as it accepted his weight. Blake took the passenger seat, resting the massive, heat-shielded barrel of the automatic shotgun against the reinforced dashboard. Marty and Bruno piled into the rear of the spacious crew cab, pulling the heavy, ballistic-steel doors shut. Atwater took the wheel of the secondary armored truck, acting as the rear guard and mobile digital command center.


Darell jammed the keys into the ignition. The massive diesel engine roared to life, a deep, powerful growl that shook the remaining dust from the barn rafters.


He didn't bother opening the main doors manually. He dropped the transmission into gear and floored the accelerator.


The heavy steel push-bumper of the F-250 slammed into the sliding wooden doors, shattering them into kindling. The armored truck tore out of the barn, tires spinning violently in the mud and slush, throwing up a massive wake of freezing water as Darell accelerated down the driveway.


They passed the burning ruins of the cedar cabin. The flames were towering higher now, illuminating the snow-dusted tree line in a brilliant, chaotic orange glow. Darell didn't look at it. He kept his eyes locked on the dark, winding access road ahead.


Blake watched from the passenger seat as Darell gripped the leather steering wheel, the patriarch's knuckles turning bone-white under the strain. The heavy ballistic plates pressed flush against Darell's chest, the suppressive weight of the assault rifle resting across his lap like an executioner's broadsword.


Darell’s jaw was locked so tightly it looked like it might shatter his molars, his ice-blue eyes fixed entirely on the icy, twisting road ahead. Blake didn't need to read his brother's mind to know exactly what Darell was broadcasting into the frozen dark. The vow was practically vibrating off the cab walls, a silent, homicidal promise echoing in the violent space between them: Hold on, wildcat. We’re coming to burn their world down.


The armored F-250 tore down the mountain, leaving the destroyed sanctuary behind, plunging into the freezing Appalachian night to unleash absolute hell on the northern ridge.


The hounds were off the leash. And they were starving.

CHAPTER 28


The Mountain's Wrath


The reinforced, heavily armored Ford F-250 did not slow down as it crested the final rise leading to Queenie Pearce’s corrugated steel warehouse. Darell Duncan kept his heavy boot planted firmly on the accelerator, the massive, modified diesel engine howling like a mechanical demon in the freezing Appalachian night.


The snow was falling in thick, blinding sheets now, illuminated only by the aggressive, high-beam halogen headlights of the truck cutting through the darkness. Ahead, the sprawling, dilapidated structure of the methamphetamine syndicate sat completely dark, its exterior floodlights extinguished by the localized cyber-attack Atwater was currently orchestrating from the secondary vehicle trailing half a mile behind them.


The cartel sicarios and Queenie's ragged militia believed the war was over. They believed the Duncans were dead, buried under the mud and limestone of the Blackwood Ravine.


They were about to discover the catastrophic consequence of failing to verify a kill.


"Brace!" Darell roared over the deafening engine noise, his hands gripping the leather steering wheel at ten and two, perfectly aligning the heavy steel push-bumper of the truck with the massive, chained loading bay doors of the warehouse.


In the passenger seat, Blake braced his boots against the floorboards, one hand securing the heavy barrel of the Atchisson AA-12 automatic shotgun, the other gripping the "oh-shit" handle above the door. In the back, Marty and Bruno locked their cores, leaning into the heavily padded seats.


Darell geared down, hitting the chained bay doors at a punishing, calculated forty miles an hour.


The impact was an apocalyptic display of brute mechanical force. The heavy steel chains securing the corrugated doors snapped instantly under the crushing weight of the armored push-bumper, the heavy links flying apart like high-velocity rifle shrapnel. The massive metal panels buckled, tearing away from their industrial tracks with a deafening, shrieking screech that overpowered the engine. The F-250 burst through the threshold, dragging tons of twisted steel and shattered concrete into the cavernous interior of the warehouse.


Darell slammed on the brakes, throwing the truck into a violent, controlled slide across the slick, chemical-stained concrete floor, stopping the vehicle perfectly perpendicular to the main processing floor, instantly establishing a heavy, armored barricade.


"Execute!" Darell commanded, kicking his door open before the truck had even fully settled.


Bruno Harding was the first man out of the rear doors. He didn't raise his rifle. He moved with a fluid, terrifying speed, his massive arm whipping forward in a wide arc. He hurled two military-grade M84 stun grenades high into the air, tossing them directly over the heads of the stunned, disorganized mass of cartel operatives and meth cooks scrambling on the floor below Queenie's elevated office.


"Flash out!" Bruno barked, dropping his chin to his chest and turning his face away from the trajectory.


Darell, Blake, and Marty instantly squeezed their eyes shut and opened their mouths to equalize the impending pressure.


The twin detonations were catastrophic in the enclosed, corrugated structure. The dual flashes of multi-million-candela light instantly blinded anyone looking in their direction, while the staggering, 170-decibel concussive blasts ruptured eardrums and completely shattered the equilibrium of the enemy forces.


A chorus of agonized screams and confused shouting erupted across the warehouse floor.


Darell stepped out from the cover of the armored door, his suppressed HK416 assault rifle already shouldered and acquired. The world was plunged into darkness again, save for the strobing effect of the tactical lights mounted to their weapons.


He didn't fire blindly into the chaos. He operated with the cold, mechanical precision of a localized extinction event. His red dot optic swept over the disoriented mass, instantly identifying the cartel sicarios by their heavy tactical gear and the silhouettes of their automatic weapons.


Crack-crack. Darell’s first double-tap struck a heavily armored operative perfectly in the exposed juncture between his Kevlar helmet and his collarbone. The man dropped instantly, his spinal cord severed.


Crack-crack.


A second operative, desperately wiping his bleeding eyes and firing his submachine gun wildly into the ceiling, took two suppressed rounds directly to the center of his faceplate, the impact snapping his head back with lethal force.


To Darell’s right, the true volume of the mountain's wrath was unleashed.


Blake Stokes stepped out from the passenger side, raised the massive AA-12, and held the trigger down. The fully automatic combat shotgun roared, a continuous, deafening thunder that completely eclipsed the suppressed fire of Darell's rifle. Blake dumped twenty rounds of 00 buckshot into the densest cluster of Queenie's militia in less than four seconds.


The devastation was absolute. The heavy lead pellets shredded the unarmored meth cooks and low-level enforcers, tearing through flesh, overturning chemical vats, and turning the center of the warehouse into a literal meat grinder. Blake didn't pause. He hit the magazine release, let the empty drum fall to the concrete, and slammed a fresh one home with a psychotic, joyous grin plastered across his face.


"Push the line!" Darell ordered, stepping away from the truck, advancing methodically across the floor. "Clear the fatal funnels! Keep moving!"


They advanced in a flawless, synergetic wedge formation. Darell took the center, providing accurate, suppressed lethal strikes against the remaining cartel assets. Blake anchored the right flank, his shotgun clearing wide swaths of the unarmored militia. Marty anchored the left, his heavy AR-15 barking in controlled, three-round bursts, suppressing the few shooters attempting to return fire from behind the rusted industrial machinery.


Bruno remained elevated, standing on the hood of the F-250, utilizing the thermal optic on his heavy .338 Lapua Magnum to identify targets hidden by the growing cloud of smoke and chemical vapor filling the warehouse.


The resistance was crumbling rapidly. The cartel sicarios were highly trained, but they had believed themselves to be the apex predators returning to a secure nest. The psychological shock of having their staging area breached by the very ghosts they thought they had killed paralyzed their tactical response. They were dying before they could even orient themselves to the threat.


"Actual, this is Overwatch," Bruno’s calm voice vibrated in Darell’s earpiece. "I have multiple hostiles attempting to secure the high ground. The mezzanine level. Approaching the primary office."


Darell shifted his aim upward. Through the settling smoke and the strobing tactical lights, he saw the raised steel catwalk leading to Queenie Pearce's faux-velvet office.


A towering, heavily built man wearing a stained denim jacket—Bo, Queenie's primary enforcer—stepped out of the office door, raising an old, pump-action shotgun.


Darell didn't even have time to squeeze his trigger.


A massive, concussive roar echoed from Bruno's position on the truck hood. The heavy, armor-piercing round from the Lapua Magnum crossed the warehouse in a fraction of a second. It struck Bo squarely in the center of his chest, the sheer kinetic energy of the impact picking the massive enforcer completely off his feet and throwing him backward through the frosted glass door of the office, shattering it entirely.


"Target neutralized," Bruno reported clinically.


"We are taking the stairs!" Darell shouted, gesturing sharply with his left hand.


He moved toward the steel staircase leading up to the mezzanine, Marty mirroring his movement on the opposite side. Blake remained on the ground floor, suppressing the remaining pockets of resistance near the loading docks.


Darell hit the first steel step, his rifle raised, his eyes scanning the shattered doorway of the office above.


Suddenly, a vicious, booming blast erupted from the top of the stairs.


A spray of heavy buckshot slammed into the steel railing directly next to Darell’s head, the ricocheting lead pellets stinging his cheek and embedding themselves into the ballistic plates covering his chest.


He dropped instantly to one knee, minimizing his profile, the instinctual reflex saving his life as a second blast tore the air exactly where his head had been a microsecond prior.


"I've got the angle," Marty growled over the comms.


Darell risked a quick glance upward.


Standing at the top of the stairs, silhouetted against the dim light spilling from the ruined office, was Queenie Pearce. Her high, teased hair was a chaotic mess, her heavy makeup smeared with sweat and panic. She was holding a sawed-off, double-barreled shotgun, her heavily jeweled hands trembling as she desperately tried to reload the breach.


She wasn't a cartel operative. She wasn't a trained killer. She was a desperate, cornered rat watching the empire she had just sold her soul to acquire burn to the ground in front of her.


"Darell Duncan!" Queenie screamed, her voice cracking, completely unhinged. She snapped the breach of the shotgun shut, raising the weapon blindly. "You're supposed to be dead!"


"I don't die on your schedule, Queenie," Darell responded softly.


He didn't stand up. He didn't try to trade fire from a disadvantaged angle. He held his position, trusting the flawless synergy of his pack.


To Darell’s left, Marty stepped completely out from behind the cover of a rusted chemical vat. The enforcer ignored the localized agony of his cracked sternum, raising his AR-15 to his shoulder with a steady, unyielding grip.


He acquired the target instantly.


Marty squeezed the trigger twice.


The sharp cracks of the 5.56 rounds echoed in the cavernous space. The first bullet struck Queenie Pearce in the right shoulder, shattering the bone and spinning her violently backward. The second round caught her precisely in the center of her chest.


Queenie let out a short, wet gasp. The sawed-off shotgun slipped from her fingers, clattering loudly against the steel grating of the catwalk. She stumbled backward, her knees buckling, and collapsed onto the mezzanine floor, sliding into the darkness of her ruined office.


The matriarch of the northern ridge was dead. The rival syndicate was officially decapitated.


"Clear," Marty reported, lowering his rifle slightly, his breathing shallow and rapid.


Darell pushed himself up from the stairs. He didn't celebrate the kill. Queenie Pearce was a symptom of the disease, a minor inconvenience that had briefly aligned with a greater threat. His singular, consuming focus remained entirely locked on the core objective.


Callie.


He ascended the stairs rapidly, his heavy boots stepping directly over the expanding pool of blood spreading from Queenie's body. He kicked the shattered remnants of the frosted glass door aside and entered the ruined office.


The faux-mahogany desk was overturned, the gaudy crystal chandelier shattered across the burgundy carpet. There was no sign of Miriam Daniel. There was no sign of Callie.


But the room wasn't entirely empty.


Cowering in the far corner, desperately attempting to squeeze herself behind a heavy, overturned metal filing cabinet, was Genevieve Hersey. The brilliant corporate architect who had orchestrated the entire war was clutching her leather portfolio, her pristine cashmere sweater covered in drywall dust, her eyes wide with genuine, uncalculated terror as she stared at the warlord she thought she had buried.


Darell didn't lower his weapon. He stepped over the debris, the muzzle of his suppressed HK416 locking directly onto the center of Genevieve’s forehead. He recognized her instantly from the digital dossiers Atwater had compiled. This was the architect. This was the spider who had spun the web in his kitchen.


"Wait!" Genevieve gasped, dropping the tablet, raising her trembling hands in the universal gesture of surrender. The cold, corporate sociopathy that had defined her existence completely fractured in the face of absolute, impending violence. "Wait, please! I have the master override codes! I can unfreeze the cartel accounts you locked! I am an asset, Mr. Duncan! I am valuable!"


Darell’s ice-blue eyes were completely black, devoid of an ounce of human mercy. He looked at the woman who had manipulated Callie's trauma, weaponized her empathy, and nearly gotten his brothers slaughtered in a frozen gorge.


"Bruno," Darell said coldly, not taking his eyes off her as the giant tracker stepped into the doorway behind him. "Secure her. Atwater is going to need those codes to finish the leak."


Bruno offered a single, terrifying nod, stepping into the room and hauling the screaming accountant to her feet with one massive hand.


"Actual, this is Grid," Atwater’s voice crackled over the radio, the digital transmission originating from his mobile command center safely staged in the secondary armored vehicle outside.


"Queenie's internal analog security cameras are entirely useless, and the severe ice storm prohibits drone deployment. However, I have successfully bypassed the closed-loop industrial HVAC control panel via their exposed exterior maintenance port. I am directly accessing the warehouse's localized environmental thermostats. The thermal sensors are detecting an isolated, heavily concentrated cluster of high-temperature body signatures moving below the primary factory floor. Subterranean level. The heat index aligns perfectly with the structural blueprints indicating a reinforced basement."


"The basement," Darell realized, his blood running cold.


"The access door is located in the northwest corner of the primary floor," Atwater directed rapidly. "It is secured by a heavy electronic deadbolt. I am initiating a brute-force override now."


Darell spun around, rushing back out onto the catwalk. He looked down at the warehouse floor. The firefight was effectively over. Blake was slowly advancing through the remaining chemical vats, executing the surviving, wounded sicarios with single, point-blank shots from his sidearm. The mountain men did not take prisoners. They did not leave threats alive behind them.


"Northwest corner!" Darell shouted down to Blake. "Move!"


Darell descended the stairs three at a time, ignoring the searing pain in his left shoulder. He sprinted across the blood-slicked concrete, navigating the bodies and the debris with lethal urgency.


He reached the northwest corner. A heavy, reinforced steel door was set flush into the cinderblock wall. A digital keypad mounted beside the handle was flashing a rapid, frantic red.


"Atwater, status," Darell demanded, gripping the handle.


"Encryption bypassed," Atwater confirmed, his voice clipping tightly. "The circuit is open."


The light on the keypad instantly flipped from red to a solid, welcoming green. The heavy, internal deadbolts retracted with a loud, mechanical thunk.


Darell didn't bother attempting to manually retract the heavy iron latch. He took a single, calculated step backward, raised his right leg, and drove the flat sole of his combat boot directly into the reinforced steel plate just beside the handle.


The heavy door exploded inward, slamming violently against the concrete wall of the stairwell with an acoustic crack that permanently warped the industrial hinges.


"Callie!" Darell roared. His voice tore down the dark, concrete shaft—a desperate, feral demand for proof of life from a man who had already watched one home burn to the ground tonight.


There was no answer.


He didn't hesitate. He plunged down the steep stairs, his heavy boots skipping steps in his frantic descent, his suppressed HK416 raised and tracking. His tactical light cut like a physical blade through the thick, mildew-scented darkness of the subterranean landing, highlighting a single, anemic incandescent bulb swinging erratically from the ceiling.


He hit the bottom floor, instantly sweeping the muzzle of his rifle across the damp, expansive basement in a tight, practiced arc.


The first thing the blinding white beam illuminated was a massive, heavily armored cartel sicario lying flat on his back near the base of the stairs. The operative was dead, his throat entirely crushed. Darell stepped over the corpse without breaking stride, his light tracking rapidly to the center of the room.


His beam locked onto a rusted metal folding chair situated directly beneath the swinging bulb. The heavy, industrial-grade plastic zip ties that had been used to bind a prisoner to the crossbar were melted, bubbling, and completely severed, hanging uselessly toward the stained concrete.


But the chair was empty.


Darell stepped onto the top landing, his suppressed HK416 raised and tracking, his tactical light cutting a harsh, blinding swath through the thick, mildew-scented darkness of the subterranean concrete shaft.


The absolute silence below was a physical weight pressing against his Kevlar plates. The localized, iron-clad control he had maintained over his own rising panic since he saw the ashes of the Copper Compound finally threatened to fracture completely. If they were too late—if the extraction team had already pulled her out through a secondary tunnel—he would have to burn the entire state to the ground to find her.


He swept the beam frantically down the steep concrete stairs, searching the pitch-black corners of the cavernous basement. His optic illuminated a rusted metal folding chair. The heavy, industrial-grade plastic zip ties designed to hold a prisoner were melted, bubbling, and completely severed, hanging uselessly toward the stained floor.


The chair was empty.


Darell stopped breathing. The sickening, hollow realization hit his chest like a physical sledgehammer.


Then, rising up from the absolute blackness of the far wall, a sound breached the silence.


"Darell."


The voice was a harsh, ragged whisper. It was fragile, wrecked by smoke and exhaustion, yet to Darell Duncan, it was the single most beautiful, stabilizing frequency in the history of the world. She was still here. She was alive.


He snapped his head toward the sound, preparing to vault down the stairs to secure her.


But before his boots could even leave the top landing, a sudden, massive whoosh of chemical ignition erupted directly in the center of the subterranean room below. The undeniable, terrifying, oxygen-sucking roar of high-proof ethanol flashing instantly into a blinding fireball illuminated the entire shaft in a hellish orange glow.


The intense thermal shockwave blasted upward, knocking Darell a half-step backward against the steel doorframe.


A split second later, the booming, concussive thunder of a heavy 9mm gunshot tore upward from the other side of the flames, vibrating the cinderblock walls and raining fine white concrete dust down onto Darell's broad shoulders.


The fear he had suppressed all night completely shattered his control. The shot hadn't come from his weapon. If the cartel was executing her in the fire, he was going to tear the shooter apart with his bare hands.


Darell roared her name, a feral, apocalyptic sound of pure desperation. He threw himself recklessly down the burning concrete stairs, his heavy boots skipping steps in his frantic, terrifying plunge into the blinding inferno, entirely unsure of the carnage that awaited him at the bottom.

CHAPTER 29


Severing the Past


Fifteen minutes before Darell Duncan kicked the heavy steel door off its hinges to find me standing over the ashes of my past, I was still sitting in the dark, calculating the exact geometry of my own death.


The timeline had fractured the moment the distant, muffled thunder of Blake’s automatic shotgun echoed through the corrugated steel ceiling above us. The massive, heavily armored sicario guarding my rusted cage didn't flinch, but his tactical attention snapped instantly upward toward the source of the chaotic noise. He pivoted slightly on the landing, his posture locking rigid as he assessed the failing security of his perimeter.


I didn't waste the precious, fleeting fraction of a second his diverted attention offered me. I had sawed through the heavy plastic zip tie, the molten edges burning into my raw wrists, the heavy silver Zippo clutched desperately in my right hand.


I lunged forward, throwing my entire body weight out of the rusted metal folding chair.


I didn't aim for the guard. He was heavily armored, standing five feet away and holding a fully automatic weapon. The mathematics of a physical confrontation were non-existent. I needed leverage. I needed chaos.


My bare feet hit the freezing, slick concrete, slipping slightly as I drove my shoulder hard into the nearest stack of rotting wooden crates situated between myself and the sicario.


The crates didn't hold weapons or ammunition. They held the seized, localized product Queenie Pearce had undoubtedly confiscated from independent moonshiners operating on the fringes of her northern ridge. They were packed with large, unmarked plastic jugs of high-proof, highly volatile, unrefined ethanol.


The crates toppled with a resounding crash. The cheap plastic jugs hit the concrete floor, shattering instantly. A massive wave of clear, suffocatingly potent liquid exploded across the basement, washing over my bare feet and rapidly expanding in a wide, slick pool directly toward the guard's position at the base of the stairs.


The sharp, intoxicating stench of raw alcohol immediately completely overwhelmed the smell of mildew and old blood.


The sicario cursed, a harsh, guttural sound, dropping his rifle from his shoulder to look down as the tidal wave of moonshine hit his heavy tactical boots. The sudden loss of friction on the smooth concrete was catastrophic. He tried to step backward, attempting to establish a firing stance, but his boots had absolutely no traction.


His feet went out from under him.


He fell hard, his massive frame crashing onto the concrete with a heavy, ungraceful thud. The assault rifle clattered out of his grasp, sliding across the wet floor, spinning wildly out of his immediate reach.


I didn't stop moving. I scrambled on my hands and knees through the freezing alcohol, my torn, oversized sweater dragging in the puddle, my raw wrists burning as the harsh chemical bit into the open wounds.


I reached the guard just as he was attempting to push himself up from the slick concrete. I didn't try to strike him. I reached past his flailing arm, my fingers wrapping desperately around the grip of the heavy, black Glock 19 holstered on his right hip.


I depressed the thumb release and yanked the pistol free.


The weapon was incredibly heavy, far heavier than I remembered the weapons in the Duncan armory feeling. I scrambled backward, putting distance between myself and the thrashing, furious operative, slipping and sliding until my back hit the solid cinderblock wall of the basement.


I brought the gun up, gripping it with both hands, my index finger slipping inside the trigger guard. My hands were shaking so violently the front sight post was a blur, but the distance was less than six feet.


"Don't move!" I screamed, my voice tearing raw from my throat.


The sicario froze, resting on his hands and knees, the ethanol soaking into the thick fabric of his tactical pants. He looked up at me, his eyes widening slightly as he registered the lethal threat. He was a professional. He recognized the erratic, unpredictable danger of a terrified, cornered civilian holding a firearm with no trigger discipline.


He didn't reach for the assault rifle. He slowly raised his hands, keeping his palms open and visible.


The heavy steel door at the top of the stairs, the one Miriam had exited through minutes prior, suddenly clanged open.


The sharp, precise click of expensive heels sounded on the landing.


"What is the cause of this disturbance?" Miriam Daniel demanded, her voice cutting through the damp air, sharp and irritated.


She stepped into the harsh light of the swinging bulb, her pristine platinum hair slightly disheveled from the concussive blasts occurring on the floor above. She had returned to the basement, abandoning her extraction protocol the moment the warehouse perimeter was breached, seeking the only leverage she believed she still possessed.


Me.


Miriam stopped at the base of the stairs, taking in the chaotic scene. She saw the overturned crates, the massive pool of spilled moonshine, the downed guard.


And then her pale, cold eyes locked onto me, cowering against the cinderblock wall, clutching the stolen Glock 19.


The mask of the corporate executioner slipped for a fraction of a second, revealing a genuine, visceral shock. She hadn't expected me to be free. She hadn't expected me to be armed.


But Miriam Daniel was a predator who had built an empire by dominating weaker psychological structures. She recovered instantly, utilizing the deeply ingrained conditioning she had beaten into me for two years.


"Put the weapon down, Callie," Miriam commanded.


Her voice wasn't loud. It didn't need to be. It possessed the exact, precise frequency of absolute authority that triggered an immediate, paralyzing response in my nervous system. It was the voice that had dictated when I ate, when I slept, and when I was allowed to speak.


My grip on the heavy pistol faltered. The barrel dipped an inch. The overwhelming, suffocating instinct to submit, to avoid the inevitable, excruciating punishment that always followed disobedience, flared hot and fast in my chest.


Miriam recognized the hesitation. She stepped forward, ignoring the spilled alcohol soaking into her expensive shoes.


"You do not possess the capacity for violence," Miriam stated smoothly, projecting absolute confidence. She raised her own hand, revealing the suppressed Sig Sauer P226 she had pointed at my head earlier. She aimed it directly at my chest. "You are an administrative asset. You are property. Drop the gun, Callie, before you force me to permanently disable you."


The guard on the floor, sensing the shift in the power dynamic, began to slowly shift his weight, preparing to lunge for his assault rifle the second I dropped the pistol.


I looked at Miriam's cold, dead eyes. I felt the familiar, terrifying pull of the cage. The urge to surrender, to accept my fate and hope for a quick death, was almost overwhelming.


But the silence in the basement was suddenly shattered by a massive, echoing roar from the stairwell.


"Callie!"


It was Darell. The patriarch’s voice tore down the concrete shaft, a dark, heavy, vibrating demand for proof of life. It was the sound of a warlord tearing the world apart to find what belonged to him.


The sound of his voice functioned like a physical shockwave, completely obliterating the psychological hold Miriam had over me. The conditioning broke. The victim died.


I wasn't the broken girl in the white penthouse anymore. I was the woman who wore the copper brand. I was the woman who had watched him take a bullet for me.


My grip on the Glock tightened. I raised the barrel, aiming it squarely at the center of Miriam’s crimson pantsuit.


"I am not your property," I spat, my voice finding a hard, jagged strength I didn't know I possessed.


Miriam’s expression darkened into absolute, homicidal rage. The realization that she had completely lost control of the asset triggered a lethal response. Her finger tightened on the trigger of the suppressed Sig.


I didn't wait for her to fire. I knew I couldn't beat her in a quick-draw shootout. She was trained. I was terrified.


I didn't lower the gun. I kept the heavy barrel of the Glock leveled perfectly at the center of her pristine crimson pantsuit. The weapon was incredibly heavy to hold one-handed, my right hand gripping the polymer frame tight enough to turn my knuckles stark white, silently daring her to make a move.


But I wasn't naive. I knew I couldn't beat a seasoned corporate executioner in a quick-draw shootout. She possessed years of tactical conditioning. I possessed pure, feral terror. I needed an absolute, undeniable equalizer.


I raised my left hand, revealing the heavy silver Zippo I had blindly snatched from the chair during my scramble and kept clutched in a death grip ever since.


My blistered thumb struck the flint wheel. The sharp, metallic clink echoed loudly over the panicked gasps of the downed guard before the brilliant yellow flame flared to life, casting a frantic, dancing light across the damp concrete walls. I didn't break eye contact with Miriam. I didn't blink. I simply opened my fingers and dropped the burning lighter directly into the center of the massive, shimmering puddle of high-proof ethanol spreading between us.


The ignition was instantaneous and catastrophic.


The entire surface of the basement floor erupted in a blinding, roaring sheet of blue and orange fire. The high-proof ethanol didn't burn slowly; it flashed, consuming the oxygen in the damp space with a terrifying, suffocating whoosh.


The wall of flames shot six feet into the air, creating a massive, blazing barrier directly between me and Miriam.


The sicario on the floor screamed, a horrific, agonizing sound, as the fire instantly engulfed his alcohol-soaked tactical pants. He rolled frantically on the concrete, abandoning his weapon entirely, desperately trying to smother the flames.


Miriam stumbled backward, throwing her arm up to shield her face from the intense, blistering heat radiating from the sudden inferno.


"You stupid bitch!" Miriam shrieked, the pristine facade completely destroyed by the primal terror of the fire.


She leveled her suppressed pistol blindly through the roaring flames, firing three rapid shots in my general direction. The bullets cracked past me, shattering against the cinderblock wall behind my head, showering me in concrete dust.


I didn't cower. I didn't drop the gun. The heat of the fire was blistering my skin, the smoke burning my eyes, but I stepped forward, closing the distance to the edge of the blazing puddle.


The flames danced wildly, momentarily parting to reveal Miriam standing near the base of the stairs, her pale eyes wide with shock, desperately attempting to clear a malfunction in her weapon.


I raised the Glock 19, gripping it tightly with both hands, aligning the sights squarely on the center of her chest. I didn't feel the recoil. I didn't hear the deafening roar of the gunshot over the sound of the fire.


I pulled the trigger.


The bullet struck Miriam Daniel dead center. The kinetic force of the impact threw her backward against the concrete wall of the stairwell. The suppressed pistol slipped from her grasp, clattering to the floor. She slumped downward, her hands clutching her ruined chest, her pristine crimson suit staining a dark, spreading black.


She looked up at me through the flames, her lips parting, a look of profound, absolute disbelief freezing on her perfect features.


Then, her eyes rolled back, and she collapsed entirely, a lifeless heap of expensive fabric and shattered arrogance lying in the dirt.


The monster was dead.


I stood frozen at the edge of the inferno, the heavy polymer frame of the Glock drooping in my trembling hands. My chest was heaving violently, sucking in the toxic, smoke-filled air as the ringing in my ears slowly began to recede. The sheer, apocalyptic surge of adrenaline that had fueled my trigger finger evaporated in a single heartbeat, leaving behind a profound, hollow exhaustion that threatened to pull my bones directly into the slick concrete. I stared blindly at the crimson-stained ruin of the woman who had terrorized my existence, waiting for the reality of her death to fully anchor in my fractured mind.


Before my knees could buckle, the heavy steel door at the top of the stairs slammed against the concrete wall with a concussive force that warped the industrial hinges.


Darell Duncan plunged into the basement like a localized god of war.


He didn't pause to assess the tactical geometry of the room. He didn't clear his corners. His ice-blue eyes swept wildly past the roaring wall of ethanol fire and the thrashing, burning operative on the floor, locking entirely onto me standing bruised and bloodied against the far cinderblock wall. The absolute, unshielded terror twisting his rugged features was a jarring, profound contrast to the cold warlord I had left behind in the living room hours ago.


He bypassed the flames entirely, vaulting over the burning, shattered crates with a massive, desperate leap that defied the heavy ballistic plates strapped to his chest. His heavy combat boots hit the wet concrete mere inches from my bare feet.


Darell didn't utter a single word. He reached out with his large, calloused hands, snatching the smoking pistol from my numb, unresisting fingers. He didn't secure the weapon; he tossed it carelessly into the shadows, removing the burden of the violence from my hands forever. Then, his massive arms clamped down over my shoulders, hauling me forcefully against the rigid ceramic armor covering his chest until every inch of me was molded to the unyielding heat of him.


He crushed me to him, his hold so fierce it bordered on bruising, completely wrapping my smaller frame in the impenetrable fortress of his body. He buried his face deep in the crook of my neck, inhaling sharply as if he could pull my very essence into his lungs. His breathing was harsh, ragged, and vibrating with an overwhelming, terrifying relief that synced perfectly with the frantic thudding of my own heart. The sharp, metallic tang of his gun oil, the rich scent of his sweat, and the underlying, grounding aroma of mountain cedar completely enveloped me, severing the nauseating stench of the cartel's blood and awakening a deep, liquid ache low in my belly.


I threw my arms around his thick neck, burying my face against the rough nylon webbing of his tactical vest, entirely ignoring the hard edges of the armor pressing into my breasts. I sobbed—not from fear, but from the shattering, absolute realization that I had fought the devil in the dark, and my wolves had still come to burn down the rest of hell to find me… to claim me with the same raw, possessive hunger now pulsing hot and heavy against my core.


A few seconds later, the heavy, frantic clatter of boots sounded on the stairs. Blake Stokes hit the bottom landing, the massive barrel of his automatic shotgun swinging wildly before coming to an abrupt halt. Blake took in the impossible, chaotic tableau: the towering wall of fire, the dead cartel queen bleeding out on the concrete, and the tight, unbreakable embrace in the far corner.


A slow, incredibly proud, feral grin spread across Blake's soot-stained face, the chaotic panic draining from his mismatched eyes, replaced entirely by a dark, reverent awe.


"Looks like the wildcat handled the extermination all by herself," Blake remarked, his voice a rich, vibrating purr carrying effortlessly over the crackling flames.


Darell slowly pulled back just enough to look at me, though he kept his left arm securely clamped around my waist, refusing to sever our physical anchor. He looked down at the blood smearing my cheek, his thumb reaching up to gently, reverently wipe a fresh tear from my soot-stained skin. The glacial detachment from the compound was gone; his eyes were dark, heavy pools of absolute, eternal devotion.


"Let's go home, little bird," Darell whispered, the sheer, vibrating relief in his deep voice washing over my exhausted soul like a baptism.


He didn't ask if I could walk, and he didn't wait for my trembling legs to inevitably fail me. Darell shifted his grip, sliding one massive arm firmly beneath my knees and the other securely around my back, lifting me completely off the freezing, blood-slicked concrete. I rested my head against the curve of his uninjured shoulder, my lungs desperately pulling in the clean, grounding scent of his skin. He carried my weight effortlessly, completely ignoring the agonizing tear of the fresh sutures in his injured deltoid, bearing me upward with the undeniable, sheltering strength of a man who had just conquered the abyss.


As he carried me up the concrete stairs, flanked by Blake and walking away from the roaring inferno that was currently reducing the architect of my trauma to ash, I didn't look back. The cage was permanently broken. The monster was dead. And I was finally, unconditionally free.
