CHAPTER 30


The Appalachian Toll (Epilogue)


The memory of Darell burying his face in my hair while Queenie Pearce’s warehouse burned to the absolute bedrock behind us—the distant, frantic wail of police sirens signaling the indisputable end of the war—was a violent ghost that had finally stopped haunting my waking hours.


It had taken exactly fourteen months for the suffocating stench of ash, melted plastic, and ozone to fully vacate my senses, overwritten entirely by the relentless, driving momentum of our resurrection. In its place, the sharp, pristine bite of freshly milled cedar and the rich, heavy perfume of fermenting sweet mash had settled permanently into my lungs, the undeniable scent of an empire reborn from the dirt.


I stood on the expansive, wrap-around porch of the newly reconstructed Copper Compound, my hands wrapped loosely around a heavy crystal lowball glass. The liquid inside was two fingers of Darell’s private reserve bourbon, aged twelve years and smooth as liquid gold. The ice cubes clinked softly against the crystal as I shifted my weight, leaning my hip against the massive, rough-hewn timber railing.


The Appalachian evening was descending with a breathtaking, crystalline clarity. The sky above the western ridge was a vast, bruised canvas of deep violet, bleeding into vibrant streaks of crushed gold and arterial red. The winter air was brutally crisp, hovering right at the freezing point, but the chill couldn't penetrate the thick, luxurious weave of the oversized cream cable-knit sweater I wore. And even if the cold had managed to slip past the wool, the heavy, polished copper teardrop resting flush against my collarbone radiated its own perpetual, grounding heat.


The brand. The shield. The undeniable proof that I belonged to the mountain, and the mountain belonged to me.


I took a slow, savoring sip of the bourbon, letting the fiery warmth trace a path down my throat, and looked down at the sprawling, orchestrated chaos of our newly expanded empire.


The physical scars of Miriam Daniel’s thermobaric strike had been entirely erased, paved over by millions of dollars of untraceable cartel capital we had systematically seized during the fallout. The new cedar cabin was a fortress masquerading as a hunting lodge, fifty percent larger than the original structure and reinforced with military-grade ballistic steel woven directly into the timber framing. The blast doors were thicker. The subterranean Hollow had been excavated deeper into the living bedrock, now housing eight towering, multi-story copper distillation columns instead of four.


We hadn't just rebuilt the sanctuary. We had expanded the kingdom.


Down in the massive, packed-dirt staging yard directly below the porch, the logistical machinery of that expansion was operating at maximum velocity.


Bruno Harding stood near the open payload doors of a massive, unmarked eighteen-wheeler, his towering frame completely dwarfing the heavy wooden pallets of vacuum-sealed product being loaded into the trailer. The tracker wasn't wearing his usual woodland camouflage today; he wore a thick, lined canvas work jacket over a dark thermal, his dark, observant eyes scanning the tree line with ingrained, perpetual vigilance. He was directing a crew of four new, fiercely vetted local men who moved with the terrified, absolute precision of soldiers who knew their employer did not tolerate incompetence.


"The load distribution is off by four hundred pounds on the rear tandem axle, Bruno," Atwater Briggs announced, stepping out of the shadows of the adjacent staging bay.


The logistics manager held a ruggedized, military-grade tablet in his left hand, his eyes fixed on the digital schematics glowing on the screen. He looked exactly the same as he had the night we broke the cartel encryption—crisp gray henley, wire-rimmed glasses flawlessly clean, projecting an aura of absolute, detached calculation.


Bruno didn't argue. He never argued with the math. He simply raised a massive, calloused hand, signaling the forklift operator to halt.


"Shift the last three pallets forward to the central support struts," Bruno ordered the crew, his deep, rumbling voice cutting effortlessly through the mechanical hum of the diesel engine. "Balance the weight. If you blow a tire on the interstate because the ass-end is dragging, I will personally throw you off the suspension bridge."


The crew scrambled to comply, the electric forklift whining as it reversed course.


Atwater looked up from his tablet, his gaze finding me on the elevated porch. A faint, almost imperceptible ghost of a smile touched the corners of his mouth. He tapped the screen of the tablet twice, locking the display, and walked toward the heavy wooden stairs leading up to my vantage point.


His boots made a quiet, rhythmic sound against the fresh cedar planks as he ascended. He stopped beside me, leaning his elbows against the railing, looking out over the sprawling yard.


"The structural integrity of the new logistics network is holding at ninety-nine point eight percent efficiency," Atwater reported, slipping immediately into the formalized, analytical cadence that had become our shared language over the last six weeks.


"And the remaining zero point two percent?" I asked, taking another sip of the bourbon, arching an eyebrow at him.


"Tire blowouts, minor mechanical failures, and the unpredictable variables of Appalachian winter weather," Atwater replied smoothly. He extended the heavy tablet toward me. "The primary eastern seaboard routes are fully operational. I require your authorization on the finalized quarterly disbursement ledger before I initiate the digital scrubbing sequence."


I set my bourbon glass down on the wide, flat top of the timber railing and took the tablet from his hands.


My fingers, completely healed from the splinters and the brutal friction burns of the zip ties, moved across the glass screen with practiced familiarity. The digital ledger Atwater had constructed was a masterpiece of illicit accounting. Miriam Daniel had attempted to freeze her own infrastructure to block our initial digital strike, but she hadn't anticipated Darell capturing Genevieve Hersey alive in the ruins of Queenie's warehouse. After Atwater spent three grueling hours in a windowless room extracting the master override codes from the terrified accountant, he had successfully forced the unredacted leak of Miriam's restored routing numbers to the federal alphabet agencies.


The resulting seizure had created a massive, catastrophic vacuum in the Mid-Atlantic underground economy. Miriam’s empire had collapsed in a matter of hours. The DEA and the FBI had descended upon her pharmaceutical warehouses and shipping ports, freezing billions of dollars in liquid capital and arresting her surviving lieutenants.


Miriam’s empire had collapsed in a matter of hours. The DEA and the FBI had descended upon her pharmaceutical warehouses and shipping ports, freezing billions of dollars in liquid capital and arresting her surviving lieutenants.


But the federal agencies couldn't seize the physical asphalt. They couldn't seize the hidden logging roads or the deeply entrenched, localized distribution hubs.


We had taken those.


With the intimate knowledge I possessed of Miriam's operational infrastructure, combined with Atwater's terrifying digital prowess and the Duncans' absolute monopoly on physical violence, we had stepped directly into the void the cartel left behind.


We absorbed Queenie Pearce’s northern ridge territory without firing a single shot; her remaining men had surrendered the moment they saw Bruno walking up the mountain carrying the massive .338 Lapua Magnum he had used to decapitate their leadership. We transitioned from supplying regional syndicates to controlling the entire eastern corridor.


I scrolled through the columns of data. Millions of dollars in untraceable, non-sequential physical capital, systematically routed through shell companies, laundered through legitimate real estate acquisitions, and reinvested into the subterranean stills beneath our feet.


I wasn't a hostage reading a ransom demand. I was the operational co-director of a multi-state syndicate, verifying the health of my own empire.


"The routing numbers for the Delaware shell corporation look slightly heavier than our initial projections," I noted, tapping a specific line item with my index finger. "Did the distribution volume increase in that sector?"


"It did," Atwater confirmed, stepping closer, his shoulder brushing comfortably against mine. "The vacuum left by Miriam's primary processing hub in that region created a desperate demand for high-proof, untraceable product. We inflated the wholesale price by fifteen percent to compensate for the accelerated logistical risk. The buyers accepted without negotiation."


"Because they know there's nowhere else to go," I murmured, a profound, undeniable thrill of pure power sliding down my spine.


I tapped the biometric authorization icon at the bottom of the screen. The tablet registered my fingerprint, flashing a solid, confirming green light, locking the ledger into the encrypted, air-gapped servers buried deep within the mountain bedrock.


"Authorized," I said, handing the tablet back to him.


"Flawless execution, as always, Callie," Atwater praised quietly. He took the device, slipping it under his arm. He looked at the side of my face, his gaze lingering on my cheekbone.


The deep laceration Miriam's diamond ring had carved into my skin had healed cleanly, leaving behind a thin, pale, crescent-shaped scar that was barely visible unless the light caught it perfectly. It was a minor imperfection, a tiny, permanent reminder of the exact moment I had stopped being a victim and started fighting back.


"You look..." Atwater started, his analytical vocabulary momentarily failing him as he searched for the correct emotional parameter. "You look completely at peace."


"I am," I answered truthfully, picking my bourbon glass back up. The ice had melted slightly, diluting the amber liquid just enough to sweeten the bite. "For the first time in two years, Atwater. I wake up, and my first thought isn't calculating the distance to the nearest exit. I'm not running anymore."


"You have no reason to run," Atwater stated, the absolute certainty of his logic acting as a structural beam supporting my reality. "The statistical probability of a hostile entity breaching this perimeter and surviving the encounter is currently zero. We hold the high ground. We hold the capital. And we hold you."


Before I could respond to the heavy, possessive warmth of his statement, the deep, synchronized roar of a massive diesel engine echoed from the winding access road leading up from the lower valley.


Bruno immediately halted the loading process in the yard, his hand dropping to the heavy hunting knife strapped to his thigh, his eyes locking onto the tree line. Atwater stiffened beside me, his posture instantly transitioning from relaxed domesticity to tactical readiness. The ingrained paranoia of the pack never truly slept; it merely rested with one eye open.


A moment later, a heavily modified, matte-black Ford F-250 crested the ridge, its aggressive halogen headlights cutting through the descending twilight. The truck was practically a tank, sitting on massive suspension lifts and armored with quarter-inch ballistic steel plating.


Bruno relaxed, his hand dropping away from the knife. He gave a short, two-fingered salute to the approaching vehicle and turned back to the forklift operator.


"Stand down," Atwater murmured, adjusting his glasses. "The hunting party has returned."


The armored truck pulled into the wide, circular driveway in front of the porch, the heavy studded tires crunching loudly against the pristine gravel. The engine died with a mechanical shudder.


The driver's side door kicked open, and Blake Stokes vaulted out of the cab.


He moved with the same chaotic, vibrating kinetic energy that had defined him since the day Darell dragged me into the basement, but the dark, feral edge of violence that had consumed him during the war had been entirely replaced by a wild, unrestrained joy. He wore a heavy Carhartt jacket over a thermal shirt, his shaggy brown hair blown in every direction by the winter wind.


He didn't walk up the porch stairs. He took them at a dead sprint, covering the distance in three massive bounds.


"Wildcat!" Blake shouted, his mismatched eyes—one emerald green, one muddy brown—blazing with absolute adoration and something far darker, far hungrier.


I barely had time to set my bourbon glass down on the railing before he hit me. Blake didn't stop his momentum; he crashed into me, his long, heavily muscled arms wrapping entirely around my waist. He lifted me completely off the cedar floorboards, spinning me around in a dizzying, breathless circle that sent my hair flying across my face and pressed every soft curve of me against the hard, insistent length of him.


"Blake, put me down!" I laughed, the sound bubbling up from the very bottom of my chest, genuine and entirely uninhibited even as liquid heat flooded low between my thighs. I grabbed his broad shoulders, holding on as the world spun and his thigh slid deliberately between mine.


"Not a chance," Blake declared, his voice a vibrating purr against my neck, teeth grazing the sensitive skin just below my ear. He let my feet touch the floor but kept me crushed flush against his chest, letting me feel exactly how hard and ready he was for me.


He leaned down, capturing my mouth in a deep, bruising, utterly unapologetic kiss. It wasn't a chaste greeting. It was a chaotic, hungry consumption—his tongue sweeping past my lips to taste the bourbon and the lingering fire of victory on my breath, his rough hands sliding under the hem of my sweater to grip bare skin, thumbs stroking possessively along my ribs. He kissed me exactly the way he had claimed me in the firelight six weeks ago—with an absolute, possessing intensity that ignored the fact we were standing in plain sight of the entire loading crew.


I didn't push him away. I sank into the contact, my hands sliding up the ballistic nylon of his jacket to completely frame his rugged, cold-flushed face, returning the heat measure for measure until my core throbbed with aching need.


When he finally pulled back, we were both breathing heavily, a cloud of white vapor mingling between our lips, his forehead resting against mine in a silent promise of the wild night ahead.


"You taste like expensive whiskey and absolute power," Blake grinned, his thumb swiping across my lower lip. "God, I missed you today. Six hours in town feels like a decade."


"You were only supposed to be gone for three," I pointed out, leaning my forehead against his chin, entirely anchored by his physical presence.


The heavy, rhythmic thud of combat boots ascending the stairs announced the arrival of the second half of the hunting party.


Marty Tyler reached the top of the porch, moving with his usual slow, deliberate, mountain-moving mass. The enforcer looked pristine. The cracked sternum that had nearly paralyzed him in the ravine had long since fully healed over the past year, the dense bone knitting back together thicker and stronger than before. He wore his signature worn denim and heavy canvas, a fresh, unlit cigar resting between his teeth.


"We would have been back in three," Marty grunted, pulling the cigar from his mouth to scowl affectionately at Blake. "If this idiot hadn't insisted on stopping at the hardware store to buy three hundred pounds of industrial Christmas lights. He says the new barn looks too tactical. Needs 'festive ambiance.'"


"It’s a compound, Marty, not a black site," Blake shot back, not releasing his grip on my waist. "We have millions of dollars and a queen. We can afford to be a little festive. I'm going to wire the entire roofline."


"If you fall off the second-story gables and break your neck, I am not resetting your spine," Atwater warned dryly from his position by the railing.


"You love me, Water," Blake winked. "You'd build me a robotic exoskeleton."


Marty ignored the banter. He stepped up beside us, his dark, deeply lined face softening as he looked at me. The harsh, intimidating enforcer who had thrown work gloves at my chest and demanded I stack cordwood until my hands bled was completely absent. In his place was the man who had taught me how to survive the dirt.


He reached out with a massive, calloused hand. He didn't pull me into a chaotic embrace. He gently cupped the back of my head, his thick fingers threading through my hair, pressing a warm, rough kiss to the crown of my head.


"You eat today?" Marty asked, his voice a low, gravelly rumble of pure protective instinct.


"I had lunch," I promised, resting my hand over his where it cupped my skull. "And Atwater made sure I drank at least a gallon of water."


"Good," Marty nodded in approval. He dropped his hand, turning his attention to the business at hand. He looked at Atwater. "The physical supply run is complete. The new generators are in the truck, and we secured the heavy-duty filtration manifolds for the expansion line."


"And the political climate?" Atwater inquired, his analytical mind immediately shifting to the strategic overview of their excursion into town.


Marty let out a dark, grim chuckle, leaning his heavy frame against the cedar support column of the porch.


"The political climate is beautifully compliant," Marty reported, crossing his massive arms over his chest. "We had a sit-down with Sheriff Miller at the diner."


I felt a sharp, involuntary spike of adrenaline at the mention of the diner—the site of the apocalyptic shootout that had initiated the war. But the spike vanished almost instantly, smothered by the absolute security of my current reality.


"How is our new Chief Law Enforcement Officer handling his promotion?" Atwater asked, a cynical edge bleeding into his precise tone.


"Miller is terrified, which makes him exceedingly efficient," Marty said. "He knows exactly how he got the badge. He knows what happened to Audrey."


Sheriff Audrey Walters. The corrupt, cynical woman who had attempted to sell our lives to Miriam Daniel for a quarter of a million dollars in cartel cash. When the dust had settled in the Blackwood Ravine, and we had realized the depth of the betrayal, Darell had not driven down the mountain to execute her. He had deployed Atwater.


Atwater had meticulously traced the serial numbers on the physical currency Miriam had used to bribe the Sheriff. He had anonymously compiled a massive, irrefutable digital dossier detailing decades of Audrey’s corruption, extortion, and illicit offshore accounts, and routed the entire packet directly to the internal affairs division of the FBI.


Audrey Walters had been arrested in her own driveway two days after the fire, her bags packed for the Gulf Coast, her drop-safe seized by federal agents. She was currently sitting in a maximum-security federal penitentiary, entirely stripped of her badge, her pension, and her freedom.


Her replacement, the young, easily manipulated Deputy Miller, had been explicitly informed by the Duncans that his continued employment—and his continued breathing—relied entirely on his absolute, unwavering blindness to the operations occurring in the southern hollows.


"Miller confirmed the state troopers have officially closed the investigation into the Blackwood Ravine," Blake added, his tone entirely casual, as if discussing the weather. "They attributed the slaughter to an internal cartel dispute over the northern expansion. Queenie Pearce’s death was ruled a casualty of the crossfire. The feds are too busy dismantling Miriam’s frozen assets on the coast to care about a pile of burned bodies in an Appalachian gorge."


"We are officially a ghost story again," Marty concluded. "The roads are clear. The county is ours."


"The county was always ours," a deep, resonant voice corrected from the threshold of the front door.


The acoustic footprint of the entire porch shifted instantaneously. Blake stopped vibrating. Marty straightened his spine. Atwater turned away from the railing. The synergetic pack instantly aligned itself around the sudden, massive gravitational pull of the patriarch.


I turned my head.


Darell Duncan stepped out of the warm, amber light of the house and onto the freezing cedar planks of the porch.


He was an absolute monolith of a man, an apex predator perfectly comfortable in the center of his domain. He wore dark, tailored denim and a heavy, charcoal-gray thermal henley that stretched tight across the massive, corded expanse of his chest and shoulders. The horrific, jagged wound that had torn his left deltoid apart in the diner had completely healed, leaving behind a thick, raised scar that only added to the terrifying architecture of his physical presence.


His dark hair was slightly damp from a recent shower, falling casually over his forehead, and his thick beard was perfectly trimmed.


Darell didn't look at his brothers. He didn't look at the massive transport truck idling in the yard, or the millions of dollars of product being loaded into the bay.


His ice-blue eyes locked entirely onto me.


The sheer, overwhelming intensity of his gaze was a physical weight, a concentrated, possessing heat that instantly bypassed the winter air and sank directly into the marrow of my bones. It was the look of a man who had burned a cartel to the ground, slaughtered a rival syndicate, and rebuilt an empire from the ashes, all for the singular purpose of keeping the woman standing in front of him safe.


"Report," Darell commanded softly, his eyes never leaving mine.


"The northern expansion is fully integrated," Atwater recited, stepping seamlessly into his role. "The quarterlies are signed. The physical supply run is complete, and the local law enforcement is entirely submissive. We are operating at maximum capacity, Actual."


"Good," Darell murmured.


He walked slowly across the porch. The space between us seemed to physically contract, the ambient energy of the evening focusing entirely on the slow, deliberate rhythm of his boots against the wood.


Blake stepped back, unwrapping his arms from my waist, seamlessly relinquishing the physical space to the patriarch. Marty and Atwater shifted slightly, forming a loose, protective semicircle around the two of us. They weren't stepping away; they were closing the perimeter.


Darell reached me. He didn't stop at a polite conversational distance. He stepped entirely into my space, his massive chest brushing against the soft wool of my sweater. He reached out with both hands, his thick, calloused fingers wrapping gently around my waist.


He didn't pull me to him. He simply anchored himself to me, establishing the physical connection that grounded his entire existence.


"You're cold," Darell observed, his voice a low, vibrating hum that resonated directly against my collarbone. He slid his hands up the sides of my ribcage, his palms incredibly hot through the thick knit of the sweater.


"I'm wearing three layers of wool, Darell," I smiled, tilting my head back to look up into his face. "And I have bourbon. I'm fine."


"You're shivering," he countered smoothly, ignoring my logic.


He shifted his weight, turning me slightly until my back was pressed flush against the heavy, immovable wall of his chest. He wrapped his massive arms entirely around me, crossing his forearms over my stomach, pulling me completely into the furnace of his body. He buried his face in the crook of my neck, his rough beard scraping pleasantly against my sensitive skin as he inhaled deeply.


The gesture was profoundly intimate, a display of absolute, unshielded vulnerability that the warlord only ever allowed within the heavily fortified confines of his own pack.


I leaned back into him, resting the back of my head against his shoulder. I felt the slow, steady, incredibly calm rhythm of his heartbeat thudding against my spine.


I looked out over the railing.


The sun had finally dipped below the jagged edge of the western ridge. The bruised purple and gold of the sky had darkened into a deep, infinite indigo, scattered with the first brilliant, icy pinpricks of the evening stars. Down in the yard, the heavy steel doors of the eighteen-wheeler slammed shut with a resounding, metallic clatter, the massive payload of illicit capital secured and ready for transit. Bruno signaled the driver, and the massive rig began to slowly crawl toward the access road, its heavy tires crunching over the frost.


The hollow was functioning perfectly. It was a well-oiled, incredibly lethal machine, completely insulated from the chaos of the outside world.


"You remember the first night I brought you here?" Darell whispered, his breath hot against my ear, his voice dropping so low it was meant entirely for me, bypassing the brothers standing mere feet away.


A sudden, sharp spike of memory flashed through my mind. The freezing cinderblock cell. The rusted bucket of water. The sheer, paralyzing terror of waking up in the dark, trapped in a cage made of concrete and steel, believing I had simply traded one monster for another.


"I remember," I murmured, my hands coming up to rest over his thick forearms, lacing my fingers through his. "You told me I owed you a toll."


Darell’s arms tightened around me, a sudden, fierce compression that spoke of a dark, lingering regret for the terror he had initially inflicted upon me. He had used fear to force my compliance, believing it was the only way to keep me from running back out into the snow and dying.


"I told you that you were indentured property," Darell continued, his lips pressing a soft, reverent kiss against the pulse point hammering furiously beneath my jaw. "I told you that you owed the house for the risk you brought to our doorstep."


I turned my head slightly, my cheek brushing against his cheekbone. "I brought the cartel. I brought the fire. I burned your cedar to the ground."


"You brought the fire," Darell agreed, his voice a dark, possessive purr. "But you also brought the sword. You handed me the ledger. You severed your own ties in the basement. You didn't break, Callie. You burned the weakness away."


He shifted his right hand, his calloused fingers moving up to my chest. He found the heavy, polished copper teardrop resting over my collarbone. He traced the smooth metal, the physical manifestation of his eternal claim, ensuring it was still perfectly in place, perfectly covering the jagged scars of my past.


"The toll is paid in full, little bird," Darell vowed, the absolute, unyielding truth of his words echoing in the quiet night. "You owe this house nothing. You own it."


He didn't need to elaborate. The proof of my ownership was standing all around me.


I looked to my right. Blake had moved to the edge of the porch, leaning casually against the railing, watching the transport truck disappear down the mountain. He caught my eye, offering a slow, chaotic wink, a silent promise of the wild, uninhibited joy that waited for me the moment the business of the day was concluded.


I looked to my left. Marty was pulling a heavy silver lighter from his pocket, striking the flint, and bringing the flame to the end of his thick cigar. The enforcer puffed rhythmically, the tip glowing cherry red in the dark. He exhaled a thick cloud of aromatic smoke, his dark eyes meeting mine with a quiet, profound respect. He was the immovable shield, the physical barrier that ensured no threat would ever breach our perimeter again.


Atwater stood nearby, his tablet tucked securely under his arm, his brilliant, calculating mind constantly sweeping the digital horizon, ensuring our invisibility remained absolute. He was the architect of our continued survival.


And below us, walking slowly up the heavy wooden stairs from the staging yard, was Bruno. The giant tracker ascended onto the porch, his massive frame radiating a calm, deeply grounded lethality. He didn't speak. He simply took his place on the perimeter, completing the circle.


I was surrounded by monsters. I was surrounded by men who had slaughtered a cartel hit squad, executed a rival syndicate boss, and orchestrated the federal dismantling of a multi-billion-dollar empire without losing a single night of sleep. They were ruthless, unapologetic predators who operated entirely outside the boundaries of human morality.


But they were my monsters.


They were the men who had stitched their own flesh together to keep me breathing. They were the men who had crawled into the dark with me, absorbed my panic, and worshiped the very scars the rest of the world found repulsive. They had taken a broken, terrified girl who only knew how to hide, and they had forged her into the matriarch of the most dangerous syndicate in the Appalachian mountains.


Darell turned me around in his arms, breaking my view of the yard.


He looked down at me, his ice-blue eyes completely devoid of the warlord's wrath, filled entirely with a heavy, consuming, and absolute devotion.


"The mountain is yours, Callie," Darell whispered, his hands sliding from my waist to cup my face, his thumbs brushing lightly over my cheekbones. "We are yours."


I looked up at the patriarch, feeling the heat of the pack radiating around me, feeling the heavy copper teardrop resting securely against my heart. The fear was completely, irrevocably gone, replaced by a dark, intoxicating, and infinite power.


"I know," I breathed, rising up onto my toes.


I wrapped my arms around his neck, pulling his head down, and captured his mouth in a deep, searing, and perfectly permanent kiss, sealing the vow under the cold, watchful eyes of the winter stars.


The toll was paid. The past was ash.


And the Copper Compound belonged to the queen.

Blake’s low, wicked laugh followed us through the heavy front door, Marty’s deep rumble close behind, and Atwater’s quiet footsteps completing the circle. No words were needed. The pack moved as one—silent, hungry, inevitable.


They carried me up the wide cedar staircase to the new master suite, the one built with reinforced beams and a bed large enough for all five of us. The room smelled of fresh pine, smoked bourbon, and the faint metallic tang of the copper accents Darell had forged himself. Moonlight poured through the floor-to-ceiling windows, painting silver across the massive four-poster bed draped in deep charcoal linens.

“Come for us, queen,” Darell commanded against my lips, voice rough with need.


I shattered with a cry that echoed off the cedar beams, body bowing as wave after wave crashed through me. They didn’t stop. They gentled me through it, then started again—slower this time, deeper. Blake replaced his fingers with his mouth, tongue working me with wicked precision while Marty and Atwater mapped every inch of my skin with lips and teeth. Darell held my gaze the entire time, ice-blue eyes blazing as he watched me come apart again and again.


When I was trembling, boneless, and glowing, they took turns claiming me properly—first Darell, slow and deep, grounding me with every powerful thrust; then Blake, wild and playful, making me laugh even as pleasure bordered on too much; Marty, steady and relentless, until I was sobbing his name; and finally Atwater, precise and devastating, drawing out every last shudder until I was floating.


Afterward, we lay in a tangled heap of limbs and sweat-slick skin, my head on Darell’s chest, Blake curled behind me, Marty’s heavy arm across my waist, Atwater’s fingers laced with mine. The copper teardrop rested warm against my skin, a constant reminder.


“You’re home,” Darell whispered into my hair, pressing a kiss to the crown of my head.


I smiled into the darkness, surrounded by my mountain kings, the fire in my veins finally at peace.


The queen had claimed her wolves.


And they had claimed her right back—body, heart, and empire.


End of Book.

CHAPTER 3


The Gilded Cage


The transition from unconsciousness to waking was not a gentle upward drift, but a violent, gasping surface. My eyes snapped open, my lungs pulling in a sharp breath that tasted aggressively of sweet corn mash and heavy, floral laundry detergent. For a fragmented second, my brain scrambled to supply a location. The silken sheets of Miriam’s penthouse? The frozen, blood-stained snow of the ravine?


Neither.


I was staring up at a ceiling made of rough, unfinished wooden beams. A single bulb, encased in a heavy wire cage, cast a sickly amber glow over the space. I didn't move my body. I just moved my eyes, scanning the periphery. Walls of flat, unpainted cinderblock. A heavy steel door with industrial hinges facing the foot of the bed. No windows. No vents, save for a small, rusted grate near the ceiling pushing out a slow stream of artificially heated air.


I was in a cage. A very clean, very warm cage.


I pushed myself up onto my elbows, and my body immediately screamed in protest. The blunt-force trauma of the car crash had settled deep into my muscle tissue during the night. My ribs throbbed with a dull, rhythmic ache, and a sharp twinge of fire lanced across my left collarbone. I reached a hand up, my fingers brushing the neckline of my dark t-shirt. The fabric was stiff with dried blood right over the jagged edges of my burn scar. The bleeding had stopped, but the skin was tight, hot to the touch, and fiercely tender.


I swung my legs over the edge of the cot. The mattress was thick and forgiving, a jarring contrast to the brutalist architecture of the room. My feet hit the concrete floor.


My boots. They had left my boots on.


A spike of pure, crystalline panic pierced through the lingering brain fog. My hands flew to my jacket. Gone. The realization hit me like a physical blow. I scrambled to pat down my dark denim jeans, a desperate, irrational reflex overriding my own short-term memory. My hands frantically mapped the empty front and back pockets of my pants before the cold, undeniable truth settled over me.


I hadn't put them in my jeans. I had shoved them deep into the pockets of the jacket I had worn into the woods. And whoever had stripped that freezing, soaked canvas from my shoulders had undoubtedly discovered my meager inventory. The silver Zippo lighter, my only source of fire, was gone. My cheap plastic burner phone, my only digital lifeline, had been confiscated. The only things connecting me to the outside world, the only meager tools of survival I had managed to scrape together, had been stripped from me while I was dead to the world.


The drive.


My breath hitched, catching painfully in my bruised chest. I dropped to my knees on the cold concrete, ignoring the sharp spike of pain in my shins. I reached down to my right boot, my trembling fingers scrambling over the scuffed, salt-stained leather. I gripped the thick, blocky heel. It was caked in dried mud and pine needles from my frantic run through the woods. I scraped the debris away with my thumbnail, my heart hammering a frantic rhythm against my ribs.


I tapped the side of the heel with my knuckle.


A dull, hollow thwack echoed in the quiet room.


I let out a shaky, desperate breath, resting my forehead against my knee for a fleeting second. The hidden compartment was still sealed. They hadn't found it. Whoever had carried me out of the snow, whoever had stripped my jacket and emptied my pockets, hadn't thought to check the structural integrity of a cheap leather boot.


Miriam’s financial empire—the ledgers, the routing numbers, the digital maps of her illicit supply lines across the eastern seaboard—was still safely tucked away in the millimeter-thin hollow of the rubber sole. It was the only thing keeping me from being a nameless victim in a shallow grave. As long as I had the drive, I had leverage. I just needed to figure out who the hell I was leveraging it against.


I pushed myself back up to my feet, my legs unsteady. Thirst hit me suddenly, a dry, abrasive sandpaper sensation coating the back of my throat. I swallowed, wincing at the friction.


I began to pace the perimeter of the room, my boots making soft scuffing sounds against the concrete. The space was roughly ten by ten. To the right of the steel door sat a small, square wooden table. On top of it was a stainless steel tray.


I approached it cautiously, my muscles coiled, half-expecting a trap.


The tray held a tall glass of condensation-beaded water, a plate with neat slices of sharp cheddar cheese, thick cuts of cured ham, and a quartered green apple. The smell of the food hit my stomach, triggering a violent, twisting pang of hunger, but my eyes bypassed the meal entirely.


Beneath the clear glass of water lay a folded piece of white paper.


I picked up the glass, the icy moisture shocking my warm fingertips. I took a small, measured sip. The water was metallic, tasting heavily of well minerals, but it was the best thing I had ever swallowed. I drank half the glass in three long gulps before forcing myself to stop, knowing I would make myself sick if I guzzled it on an empty stomach.


Setting the glass down on the tray, I picked up the paper and unfolded it.


The handwriting was aggressive. Sharp, deeply indented angles etched in black ink, written with a heavy hand that suggested the author didn't care if the pen tore through the page.


Eat. You aren't leaving. There was no signature. Just a single, slashed line under the words that felt like a localized tremor of authority.


I crushed the paper in my fist, my jaw tightening. You aren't leaving. It was the exact same sentiment Miriam used to whisper in my ear while adjusting the cuffs on my wrists. The geography had changed. The temperature had changed. But the reality remained a suffocating constant. I was property. I had merely traded a sleek, high-tech cartel penthouse for a subterranean concrete box in the Appalachian mountains.


I threw the crumpled paper onto the floor. I wasn't going to submit to the psychology of the cage. Not again.


I picked up a slice of the apple, biting into it sharply. The tart juice flooded my mouth, waking up my sluggish senses. I ate the ham and the cheese, forcing the calories down, demanding my body convert the protein into energy. I needed strength. If I was going to manipulate my way out of this, or fight my way through that steel door, I couldn't do it running on fumes and frostbite.


After finishing the food, I resumed my sweep of the room. I checked the heavy hinges of the door. They were welded on the outside. There was no internal doorknob, just a flat steel plate and a keyhole that didn't penetrate all the way through to my side. Completely impenetrable without heavy machinery or the right key.


I moved to the cot, dropping to my hands and knees to inspect the underside. The metal frame was bolted directly into the concrete floor. No loose bars. No hidden sharp edges I could pry loose to fashion a shiv.


I crawled along the edge of the wall, dragging my fingertips along the seam where the cinderblocks met the floor. I was looking for a draft, a loose mortar joint, anything that indicated a structural weakness. I reached the back corner, right behind the head of the cot, and my index finger caught on a sharp, jagged edge of concrete.


I paused, leaning closer. The shadow cast by the cot obscured the corner, but as my eyes adjusted, I saw a subtle irregularity. A single cinderblock, down at the baseboard level, was slightly discolored, resting a fraction of an inch deeper into the wall than the others around it.


I hooked my fingernails into the tiny gap between the blocks and pulled.


It didn't budge. I shifted my weight, bracing my left hand against the floor, and dug the fingers of my right hand deeper into the crevice. I gritted my teeth, ignoring the stinging pain as a fingernail bent backward and snapped, a hot bead of blood welling at the cuticle. I pulled with my shoulder, using the leverage of my entire body.


With a grinding grate of stone on stone, the block shifted.


It wasn't a full block. It was a facade, a hollowed-out cap of concrete resting over a small, square recess in the wall. I pulled the cap away, setting it silently on the floor, and leaned in to inspect the dark cavity.


Dust coated my fingers as I reached inside. My hand brushed against something cold, ridged, and distinctly metallic.


I paused, leaning closer, my eyes straining against the heavy amber gloom. Recessed deep into the shadow of the cinderblock wall was a small, square industrial ventilation duct, secured by four rusted Phillips-head screws. A steady, faint stream of cool, ozone-tinged air pushed through the slats.


I pressed my ear directly against the freezing metal grate, holding my breath. The ductwork functioned like an acoustic funnel, carrying the muffled, ambient noise from a room further down the subterranean corridor. I could hear the distinct, high-pitched hum of heavy-duty server cooling fans, followed by the rhythmic, rapid-fire clatter of someone typing furiously on a mechanical keyboard. Whoever had stripped me of my jacket was sitting just down the hall, tearing into the digital footprint I had carried onto their mountain.


Suddenly, the typing stopped. A sharp, digital chirp echoed down the metal ductwork, the undeniable sound of an external audio file being extracted and loaded into a media player.


"Bypass successful. Playing isolated audio packet now," a man’s voice murmured, his tone analytical and terrifyingly calm.


A heavy, electronic beep resonated through the vent, carrying perfectly down the duct.


Then, a voice began to play through the unseen computer speakers down the hall.


The blood instantly drained from my face, leaving my skin clammy and completely devoid of warmth. My heart plummeted into my stomach.


It was Genevieve.


The audio was slightly distorted by the acoustics of the metal shaft, but the frantic, breathless terror pitching her voice an octave higher was unmistakable. When my captors had emptied my pockets, they hadn't merely confiscated the burner phone; they had handed it to a digital architect. The moment the device was hardwired into their secure grid to strip its encryption, it had caught a microscopic sliver of cellular bandwidth. My knees went weak. My knees went completely weak, the structural integrity of my legs failing beneath the crushing, localized gravity of that familiar voice. It was Genevieve.


Her voice was high, pitched with a frantic, breathless terror that I had never heard from her before. Genevieve was the calm one. She was Miriam's chief accountant, the woman who handled millions of dollars of blood money without ever losing her polite, professional smile. She was the one who had quietly slipped me the encrypted drive. She was the one who had left the side door of the estate unlocked.


"Callie, please tell me you have this phone on," Genevieve's recorded voice trembled, accompanied by the muffled sound of a door slamming in the background. "She knows. Oh god, Cal, she knows the safe is empty."

Anyone who brings the drive back gets five. You have to call me, Cal. Tell me where you are. I have a clean car. I can get you out of state, but you have to tell me where you ran. Please. "End of message."


The horrifying recording cut off with a sharp, synthetic beep, instantly severing the connection. The unseen computer speakers down the hall abruptly went dead, leaving only the soft, ambient hiss of the continuous, ozone-laced air pushing through the heavy metal ductwork.

Genevieve was trapped. Because of me. I had taken the drive to buy my own freedom, and I had left her standing in the blast radius of Miriam's psychotic rage. If I stayed in this cage, if I played the defiant prisoner for these mountain men, Genevieve was going to die. Miriam would eventually audit the security logs. She would find the discrepancy in the door codes. She would execute Genevieve just as coldly as she had executed Marcus.


I couldn't just survive anymore. I had to get out. Today. I had to get to a live phone, call Genevieve, and use the leverage on my boot to call off Miriam's dogs.

Someone was coming for me.


My eyes darted around the bare room, searching for anything I could use to equalize the power dynamic. The bed was bolted. The bucket was too light.

CHAPTER 4


Bad Cop, Worse Cop


Blake Stokes allowed his teasing, baritone drawl to hang in the damp basement air, entirely unfazed by the lethal standoff. His internal, hyper-aroused nervous system violently calibrated to the threat level, but he remained locked securely onto the magnificent, fiercely trembling disaster of torn denim and dried blood currently pressed flat against his unpainted cinderblock wall.


Holding his ground against the doorframe, Blake deliberately dropped his arms, absorbing the blistering, unadulterated hostility radiating from her small frame like a starving addict soaking up a fresh, intoxicating hit of pure adrenaline. She was supposed to be a broken, half-frozen liability waiting for execution; instead, she was vibrating with the feral intensity of a live explosive.


He expected her to hurl an insult. He expected her to scream for Darell.


She didn't offer a single syllable of verbal rebuttal. Instead, the frantic, cornered-animal terror radiating from her small frame instantly condensed into pure, unfiltered kinetic violence. Blake’s highly trained peripheral vision caught the subtle, desperate shift of her right arm. The exact second he let his lazy smirk stretch fully across his jaw, she whipped her hand forward, revealing the heavy ceramic diner plate, and drove the thick edge of the porcelain forcefully backward, smashing it with devastating leverage against the jagged mortar directly behind her shoulder.


The sharp, concussive crack of the diner-issue porcelain shattering echoed like a gunshot in the damp basement. Shards of white ceramic rained down onto the freezing concrete.

Her knuckles were stark, bloodless white where her right hand gripped the massive, razor-sharp triangular wedge. He took one smooth, flawlessly balanced predatory step deeper into her cage, the thick rubber soles of his heavy work boots making absolutely no sound on the freezing concrete.


Her breathing was a shallow, rapid staccato, hitching her ribcage up under the thin, sweat-dampened material of her dark t-shirt, telegraphing the frantic, cornered-animal adrenaline flooding her veins. He watched the sharp point of the ceramic track the center of his chest, matching his every micro-movement.

Blake pinned her right arm against the stone above her head, his grip unyielding but careful not to crush the delicate bones. He stepped into her guard, pressing his heavy, muscular chest flush against hers, trapping her left arm between their bodies until every hard plane of him molded to her softer curves.


The ceramic shard slipped from her suddenly numb fingers, shattering into useless white dust against the concrete floor.


"Gotcha," Blake whispered, his face inches from hers, breath hot and ragged.


She thrashed, a violent, full-body buck meant to throw him off, but he didn't budge an inch. The physical disparity between them was absolute—his hundred pounds of solid muscle forged in the hollows pinning her like living steel. Each desperate twist only ground her hips harder against the thick ridge of his arousal, sending a molten ache spiraling low through her belly. He absorbed her panic with a low, vibrating chuckle that rumbled straight into her bones, his mismatched eyes locking onto hers with raw, unfiltered hunger.


Up close, she smelled like fear, copper blood, and the biting cold of the winter woods. The dark stain on her collarbone hummed with fresh heat against the furnace of his chest. He felt the frantic, rabbit-fast thumping of her heart, echoing the heavy pulse of need building between them.


"Get off me," she hissed, baring her teeth even as her body betrayed her with a traitorous shiver.


"Now, why would I do that?" Blake murmured, leaning his weight just a fraction heavier against her, letting her feel every inch of his solid heat and the unmistakable hardness pressing insistently at the apex of her thighs. "You invited me in for a dance, wildcat. Seems rude to end it before the music stops… before I feel exactly how wet that fire in you really is."


He watched her pupils dilate, the hazel irises swallowed by black. She wasn't just scared; she was calculating. Her eyes darted to the thick corded muscles of his neck, searching for an artery to bite. The sheer, unadulterated violence in her expression made a hot, heavy pool of desire coil low in Blake’s gut. Darell wanted answers. Blake just wanted to keep her.


"You're trespassing on Duncan land," Blake said, his voice dropping an octave, the flirtatious teasing edging into something darker and far more territorial. "You saw the stills. You saw the operation. In my world, that means you don't get to make demands. It means you belong to the house."


She went perfectly still. The frantic thrashing stopped, replaced by a rigid, terrifying tension. "I don't belong to anyone."


"Everybody belongs to somebody," a new, gravel-rough voice boomed from the hallway.


Blake sighed, a small puff of air brushing against the woman’s bruised cheek. He didn't step back, but he turned his head as a massive shadow blotted out the light from the corridor.


Marty Tyler filled the doorway.


If Blake was the live wire of the syndicate, Marty was the anvil. He was broad-shouldered, thick-chested, and wore a permanent scowl etched into his weathered features. He smelled like cheap black coffee, gun oil, and fresh-cut lumber. Marty didn’t play games. He was the enforcer, the blunt instrument Darell pointed at problems until they stopped moving.


"You gonna play with your food all day, Stokes, or are we gonna get to work?" Marty grunted, stepping fully into the cell. He kicked the shattered remnants of the ceramic plate out of his way with the steel toe of his work boot.


Blake finally took a step back, releasing her wrist.


The woman instantly slumped against the cinderblock, her chest heaving as she sucked in the damp basement air. She crossed her arms over her chest, her hands curling into tight fists, her eyes darting between Blake and Marty. The dynamic in the room had fundamentally shifted. The space felt infinitely smaller, compressed by the suffocating weight of two predators flanking a single exit.


"Just establishing ground rules, Marty," Blake said cheerfully, shoving his hands deep into the pockets of his dark jeans. He leaned his hip against the small wooden table, entirely relaxed. "She likes to throw tableware. I was just teaching her some manners."


Marty didn’t smile. He stepped closer to the woman, towering over her. She had to tilt her head back to maintain eye contact, but to her credit, she didn't look away. She didn't cower.


"Darell said you had a brand on you," Marty stated, his voice flat, completely devoid of Blake's playful cadence. "Said you looked like you'd been running a long time before you put that sedan into a pine tree."


"I hit a patch of black ice," she lied smoothly, her voice gaining a fraction of its strength back. "I was just passing through. I don't know anything about your stills. Let me walk out of here, and you'll never see me again."


Marty let out a harsh, barking laugh that held zero humor. "Passing through. On a dead-end logging road. In the middle of a blizzard. With a burner phone and a Zippo lighter, but no wallet, no ID, and no registered plates on a stolen car."


Marty leaned down, placing a massive, calloused hand flat against the cinderblock right beside her head, boxing her in. "You think we're stupid, girl? You think 'cause we live in the dirt we don't know how to read the tracks?"


She swallowed hard, but her chin tilted up a fraction of an inch higher. "I don't care what you read. I'm telling you, I'm nobody."


"Nobody doesn't drive a sixty-thousand-dollar stolen Audi into the ravine," Marty countered, his voice dropping to a menacing rumble. "Nobody doesn't have professional burn scars on their chest. Someone is looking for you. And if they follow your blood trail onto our mountain, they become our problem. I don't like problems."


Blake watched the exchange with hungry fascination. He was the pressure release valve, the chaotic 'good cop' to Marty's relentless, grinding interrogation. It was a routine they had perfected over the years, usually employed on rival moonshiners caught tapping their supply lines. But this was different. The girl wasn't breaking.


"Come on, Marty, don't yell at her," Blake chimed in, stepping off the table and moving to stand just behind Marty's shoulder. He shot her a bright, conspiratorial wink. "She’s had a rough night. Wrecked her nice ride, froze her toes off, woke up in a concrete box. Cut her some slack."


"I'll cut her throat if she brings a cartel hit squad down on Atwater and Bruno," Marty snapped back, though he didn't take his eyes off the girl. "Who are you running from?"


"No one," she insisted, her knuckles turning white where her arms were crossed.


Marty stepped closer, erasing the last few inches of personal space. The sheer physical intimidation tactic was usually enough to make hardened criminals start stuttering. "Wrong answer. Try again. Is it the Cartel? Is it Queenie Pearce’s crew up on the northern ridge? Who owns the brand on your chest?"


"I said, no one!" she shouted, a flash of pure, terrified rage breaking through her composed facade.


"We can do this the easy way, darlin', or we can let Marty here do it his way," Blake purred, his mismatched eyes tracking the rapid pulse beating at the base of her throat. "And Marty doesn't have much of an imagination. He just breaks things until they fit into the boxes he likes. Tell us the name. We can protect you. Ain't nobody coming up this mountain that we can't put in the dirt."


"You can't protect me!" she spat, her voice cracking with a desperate, bitter edge. She shoved hard against Marty’s chest with both hands. It was like pushing against a brick wall; the big man didn't move a millimeter. "You have no idea what you're dealing with! You're just a bunch of backwoods thugs hiding in a cave. You have no idea what she is capable of!"


The echo of her shout bounced off the concrete walls and died instantly.


The silence that followed was absolute, heavy, and freezing cold.


Blake’s smile vanished.


Marty slowly pulled his hand away from the cinderblock wall, standing straight up. The grumpy, intimidating act dropped away, replaced instantly by the cold, calculating focus of a syndicate enforcer who had just found the loose thread.


Blake watched the exact, microscopic fraction of a second where the lethal trap snapped shut around her throat. He didn't need to read her mind to see the devastating realization crash into her; her physiological reaction broadcasted the error louder than a federal siren. Her breath hitched with a sharp, audible gasp, and those magnificent, defiant hazel irises blew completely wide, swallowed entirely by a bottomless, pitch-black horror.


She snapped her mouth shut so fast her teeth clicked together, her jaw locking into a rigid, trembling line of absolute panic as she desperately, uselessly tried to swallow the lethal pronoun back down into her lungs. She pressed her hands flat against the wall behind her, as if trying to physically merge with the stone to escape the words hanging in the air.


"She," Marty repeated. The single pronoun dropped from his lips like a lead weight.


Blake tilted his head, his chaotic energy crystallizing into razor-sharp, tactical interest. They had assumed a husband, a pimp, a rival gang boss. A man. The entire Appalachian underworld was run by men, save for Queenie Pearce, and Queenie didn't use branding irons. A female shot-caller capable of inspiring this level of abject, suicidal terror in her victims meant they were dealing with a completely different breed of monster.


"Well, well, well," Blake murmured softly, the southern drawl bleeding out of his voice entirely. He stepped around Marty, closing the distance until the toes of his boots bumped against hers. He reached out, his long fingers gently catching her chin before she could turn her face away. He tipped her head up, forcing her to look into his mismatched eyes.


Her skin was ice cold, but the fire in her was still burning, trapped and desperate.


"You just changed the whole game, wildcat," Blake whispered, his thumb lightly brushing the bruised line of her jaw. "A woman chasing a woman into the frozen mountains. That sounds like a blood feud. That sounds like cartel money."


"Don't touch me," she breathed, though she didn't try to pull away from his grip. The physical contact seemed to anchor her, even as it terrified her.


"Marty," Blake said, not breaking eye contact with the girl. "Go tell Darell. Tell him our little stray didn't run from a king. She ran from a queen."


Marty didn't argue. He gave the girl one last, heavy look before turning on his heel. His heavy boots thudded against the concrete as he walked out of the cell, leaving the heavy steel door standing wide open to the illuminated hallway.


Blake let his hand drop from her chin. He took a slow step backward, giving her room to breathe, but making sure his presence still dominated the small space. The power dynamic had shifted irrevocably. She wasn't just a captive anymore; she was a high-value intelligence asset. And she knew it.


"You shouldn't have said that," Blake told her, his voice devoid of the teasing lilt. He was deadly serious now. "Darell isn't going to let you go now. Not ever. You just proved you have a secret worth killing for."


She stared at him, her chest heaving, the defiance in her eyes warring with the crushing realization that she had just trapped herself deeper in the snare.


"Who is she?" Blake asked, his voice a low, demanding hum.


She set her jaw, her lips pressing into a thin, white line. She wasn't going to give up the name. Not yet. She was going to fight for every inch of ground.


Blake felt that familiar, unhinged grin crawling back onto his face. He took one final, lingering look at the magnificent fire trapped in her hazel eyes before taking a deliberate step backward, gracefully retreating over the threshold. It was going to be a hell of a long, brutal winter on the mountain, and he was going to enjoy every single second of breaking her open. He grabbed the heavy iron handle, pulling the steel door firmly shut behind him.

CHAPTER 5


The Toll


The heavy deadbolt slid into place with a resounding, mechanical clack, the sheer weight of the iron vibrating straight through the damp cinderblock walls of the subterranean corridor as Blake locked the cell.


Darell Duncan stood perfectly still in the flickering, abrasive buzz of the fluorescent hallway lighting, his broad shoulders relaxed but his mind operating at a hyper-vigilant frequency. He listened intently to the auditory footprint of the corridor, tracking the heavy, retreating footfalls of his brothers as they moved toward the wooden stairs.


Marty walked with a flat, punishing rhythm, his rage radiating outward like a slow-burning coal fire. Blake’s steps were lighter, trailing a few seconds behind, bouncing with the chaotic, unhinged energy of a hunting hound that had just tasted its first drop of fresh blood. Darell waited until both sets of boots hit the upper floorboards, leaving him entirely alone in the suffocating silence.


He leaned his broad shoulder against the cool, painted concrete. The heavy ring of iron keys hung from his right hand, the metal biting a familiar, grounding groove into his calloused palm. He closed his ice-blue eyes for a fraction of a second, inhaling deeply. The air down here was artificially dry, scrubbed through the heavy-duty HVAC system that kept the cavernous distillery operations adjacent to this corridor from suffocating the entire house in ethanol fumes. But beneath the ozone and the faint, sweet decay of corn mash, Darell could smell the sharp, metallic tang of pure, unfiltered terror seeping out from under the steel door.


She was terrified.


Good. Terror was a baseline. Terror was something Darell could work with. It was the arrogance he couldn't tolerate.


He opened his eyes, pushing his weight off the wall. The slip of the tongue Blake had goaded out of her had changed the entire topography of the board. She. A female shot-caller. Darell had spent his entire life navigating the treacherous, blood-soaked waters of Appalachian organized crime. He knew every player from the West Virginia coal lines down to the Kentucky borders. Women in this life were rare. Women who commanded the kind of fear that made a girl drive a stolen luxury sedan into a freezing ravine were practically mythological.


Except for one.


Atwater hadn't needed to crack the heavily encrypted partition on the burner phone to find the answer. He had simply pulled the unencrypted ping logs from the localized cell tower the moment the device had caught a signal. The phone had immediately attempted to handshake with a private, heavily shielded server cluster located in a penthouse suite three hundred miles east. A server cluster registered to a shell corporation that Atwater had been tracking for two years, an entity that controlled a massive, violently expanding cartel operation encroaching on the mountain territories.


Miriam Daniel.


Darell’s jaw tightened, the muscles ticking beneath his dark, neatly trimmed beard. Miriam Daniel wasn't a rival moonshiner. She wasn't a local problem. She was a corporate, sociopathic apex predator who gutted her competition and hung them from overpasses. And the bruised, bleeding, defiant wildcat locked inside his holding cell had just dragged Miriam’s shadow directly onto Duncan land.


Darell lifted his right hand. He slid the heavy brass key into the mechanism. He didn't rush. He let the metal scrape loudly against the tumblers, broadcasting his intent. He turned the key. The lock disengaged with a heavy thud.


He pushed the door open and stepped into the amber gloom of the cell.


Callie was exactly where Blake and Marty had left her. She was pressed flat against the back wall, her spine rigid, her chin tucked defensively. The thin, sweat-dampened material of her ruined dark t-shirt clung desperately to her small frame, doing absolutely nothing to insulate her against the subterranean chill or hide the violent, rhythmic shudder wracking her bruised shoulders.


The shattered white dust and jagged fragments of the ceramic plate she had just tried to drive straight through Blake’s throat coated the scuffed leather toes of her heavy boots. She looked completely stripped down, a frayed nerve ending exposed to the abrasive air of the cell, but the defiant, lethal geometry of her posture remained entirely intact.


Darell didn't look at the broken plate. He didn't look at the spilled food. He looked only at her.


He pulled the heavy steel door shut behind him. He didn't lock it. He didn't need to. The psychological barrier he brought into the room was far more impenetrable than an iron deadbolt.


"You've had a busy morning," Darell said. His voice was a low, resonant rumble, a slow Appalachian drawl that filled the small ten-by-ten room without him ever needing to raise the volume. It was the voice of a man who never had to repeat a command.


Callie swallowed hard. Her hazel eyes, wide and completely blown out with residual adrenaline, tracked his every micro-movement. She didn't speak. She kept her hands balled into tight fists at her sides, ready to fight a war she had already lost.


Darell walked over to the small wooden table. He placed the heavy ring of keys down on the surface. The iron clinked sharply against the wood. He unbuttoned the cuffs of his dark denim shirt, slowly rolling the sleeves up to his elbows, exposing the thick ropes of muscle and the faded copper-still tattoos wrapping around his forearms.


"My brothers lack a certain... delicacy," Darell continued, turning his body fully toward her. He crossed his arms over his chest, his boots planted firmly shoulder-width apart. "Blake thinks everything is a game designed for his amusement. Marty thinks every problem is a nail waiting for a hammer. They have their uses. But they don't make the decisions in this hollow. I do."


"I told them," Callie rasped, her voice torn and defensive. "I'm not bringing anyone here. I just want to leave."


"You aren't leaving," Darell stated. It wasn't a threat. It was an immutable law of physics. Gravity pulls down. Water flows downhill. Trespassers do not walk away from the Copper Compound. "You seem to be operating under a severe misunderstanding of your current geography, little bird. You aren't in a motel. You aren't in police custody. You are standing inside a fortress that protects a multi-million dollar illicit operation. An operation you have now seen with your own two eyes."


"I didn't see anything," she shot back, a desperate, frantic lie. "It was dark. I was unconscious."


Darell tilted his head a fraction of an inch. "You saw the reinforced gates. You saw the armed tracker in the woods. You smell the mash seeping through the vents. You know enough to put a federal target on my back. And out here, we don't rely on the law to protect our investments. We rely on dirt. Six feet of it, usually."


Callie flinched, her shoulders drawing up toward her ears. The fight in her was a brilliant, beautiful thing, but it was running on fumes. She was exhausted, battered, and starving.


Darell unfolded his arms. He took one slow, deliberate step toward her.


She pressed backward, but the cinderblock offered no retreat.


"But I am a pragmatic man," Darell murmured, taking another step, systematically erasing the physical distance between them. The smell of him—rich, dark, and overwhelmingly masculine—washed over her, a sensory anchor in the sterile room. "I don't waste useful resources. And you, Callie Schuman, are an incredibly useful resource."


Her breath hitched audibly. The sound of her own name, spoken aloud in this concrete box, struck her like a physical blow. Her eyes darted wildly, searching his stoic, unreadable face.


"How do you..." she started, her voice breaking.


"You think a burned SIM card stops Atwater?" Darell asked softly. He took the final step, stopping barely six inches from her. He was close enough to see the rapid, frantic flutter of her pulse beneath the translucent skin of her throat. He towered over her, forcing her to tilt her head back to maintain eye contact. "It took him less than an hour to pull the routing data. To find the shell companies. To find the name of the woman who owns the penthouse you ran from."


Callie stopped breathing entirely.


"Miriam," Darell said, dropping the name into the space between them like a live grenade.


The reaction was instantaneous and devastating. Callie’s knees buckled. It was a microscopic collapse, a sudden loss of structural integrity, but Darell caught it. He reached out with lightning speed, his large, calloused hands clamping down over her shoulders, holding her upright against the wall.


"Don't fall," he commanded, his grip firm, holding her together when her own body wanted to shatter. "You don't get to fall apart. Not yet."


"She's going to kill you," Callie whispered, the words tumbling out in a rush of pure, white-hot panic. The defiance was gone, replaced by the absolute, crushing certainty of a survivor who understood the true scale of the monster hunting her. "You don't understand. She isn't a gang. She's a corporation. She has politicians. She has an army. If you know who she is, you have to let me go. If she tracks me here, she will burn this mountain to the bedrock. She will slaughter every single one of you just to make a point."


Darell didn't let go of her shoulders. He leaned in closer, his ice-blue eyes boring into her panicked hazel ones.


"Let her try," Darell rumbled, the sheer, unapologetic arrogance in his tone cutting straight through her hysteria. "Let Miriam Daniel bring her suits and her city sicarios into my woods. They don't know the terrain. They don't know the hollows. We have been holding this territory in blood for three generations. No cartel queen is going to take it from me."


He felt the tremors wracking her frame lessen slightly, grounded by the absolute, unshakable conviction radiating from his massive frame.


"But that brings us to your debt," Darell continued, his voice dropping into a smooth, hypnotic cadence. He released her left shoulder, bringing his hand up to brush a tangled lock of dirty-blonde hair away from her damp forehead. The gesture was shockingly gentle, a jarring contrast to the violent reality of their situation. "You brought a war to my doorstep. You consumed our resources. You saw our secrets. There is a toll for that."


Callie stared at him, her lips parted, completely disarmed by the gentle touch. "I don't have any money."


"I don't want your money," Darell said. He stepped in closer, his heavy chest brushing against hers, pinning her psychologically to the stone. "I want your obedience. I want your labor. You are going to earn your air in this house."


He let his hand slide down from her forehead, his thumb tracing the sharp, tense line of her jaw.


"You belong to the crew now," Darell stated, laying down the ultimate law of the Copper Compound. "You work for Atwater. You clean for Marty. You entertain Blake when he requires it. You follow Bruno's path when you walk outside. You are ours. We share the burden of this empire, and we share the spoils. You are no longer a runaway. You are indentured property. If you try to run, Bruno will track you down, and I will put a bullet in your kneecap to ensure you never take another step without my permission. Do you understand the terms?"


Callie’s chest heaved violently against his solid, immovable frame. Darell watched the realization fracture the last remaining walls of her defiance. He wasn't offering her a quiet, independent sanctuary; he was offering her the terrifying, absolute security of the pack. She was being pulled directly into the center of a heavily armed, multi-headed beast. He could see the frantic calculations racing behind her hazel eyes as she processed the gravity of his terms—the terrifying transition from being hunted by a solitary dictator to being claimed, entirely and irrevocably, by a fiercely territorial council of wolves. She would share their roof, their rules, and their blood, bound by a localized law forged entirely in steel and copper.


"You're monsters," she breathed, a tear finally breaking free, cutting a clean track through the soot on her cheek.


"We are survivors," Darell corrected softly.


He moved his hand down, his long, rough fingers sliding beneath the collar of her borrowed flannel shirt. He found the jagged, angry edge of the burn scar resting over her left collarbone.


Callie flinched violently, a sharp gasp tearing from her throat as she anticipated the agonizing, searing pain Miriam always delivered. She squeezed her eyes shut, turning her face away, bracing for the punishment.


The pain never came.


Instead, Darell’s calloused thumb stroked gently, reverently, over the damaged, raised tissue. The touch was warm, grounding, and entirely devoid of cruelty.


Callie’s eyes fluttered open in shock. She looked back at him, her breath catching in her throat.


Darell was staring down at the scar, his jaw set in a line of pure, lethal fury. The anger wasn't directed at her. It was directed at the ghost of the woman who had held the iron.


"She marked you," Darell whispered, his voice vibrating with a dark, territorial possessiveness that made the fine hairs on the back of Callie's neck stand up. "She thought she could brand you like cattle. She thought she owned you."


Darell shifted his gaze back up to meet her eyes. The cold calculation was gone, replaced by a heavy, burning intensity that pinned her to the spot more effectively than any physical restraint.


"Listen to me very carefully, Callie," Darell commanded, his thumb still resting gently over the scar, tracing slow, reverent circles that sent liquid heat pooling low in her core. The rough pad of his finger ignited every nerve ending along the raised tissue, turning old pain into something dark and achingly alive. "While you are in my house. While you belong to my crew. No one else touches you. No one else raises a hand to you. And absolutely no one will ever leave another mark on this skin."


Callie stared at him, her mind short-circuiting. The sensory input was a massive, contradictory overload—his calloused thumb branding her with possessive fire while his massive frame loomed like a living wall of heat and muscle. He was a captor dictating terms of absolute slavery, threatening to shoot her if she ran. Yet, the heavy hand resting against her chest felt like the safest, most impenetrable shield she had ever known, each slow stroke coaxing a helpless throb of need between her thighs.


"You are ours to keep," Darell promised, leaning down until his lips were a fraction of an inch from her ear, his warm breath ghosting over her skin like a promise of sin. "And we do not let anyone break what is ours. You submit to us, and I swear to you on my father's grave, I will build a wall of cartel corpses so high Miriam Daniel will never be able to see over it… while I claim every trembling inch of you until the only name you remember is mine."


The absolute authority in his vow shattered the last remaining wall of Callie’s resistance. The crippling, paralyzing terror of Miriam's reach suddenly fractured. The monster under her bed had just been eclipsed by the devil standing right in front of her. And the devil was offering her sanctuary, for a price.


Darell slowly pulled his hand away from her collarbone. He took a deliberate step backward, giving her room to breathe, re-establishing the physical boundary between master and subject.


He didn't wait for her to verbally agree. He didn't need to. He could see the structural shift in her eyes. The transition from prey to protected property was already taking root in the fertile soil of her trauma.


Darell turned his back on her. He walked to the small wooden table, picked up the heavy iron ring of keys, and walked toward the open doorway.


"I'll have Atwater bring you some fresh clothes," Darell said, pausing at the threshold, his broad back blocking the light from the hallway. "Get cleaned up. Tonight, you eat upstairs with the family."


He stepped out into the corridor. He grasped the heavy iron handle and pulled the steel door shut. The deadbolt clicked into place with a resounding, final clack.


Darell stood in the quiet hallway for a long moment, the heavy keys swinging gently in his grip. The war was coming to the mountain. He could feel it in the marrow of his bones. Miriam Daniel would not let her prize vanish into the Appalachian dirt without a bloodletting.


But Darell Duncan had never lost a war on his own soil. And he was very much looking forward to the harvest.
